*Albert Heekwan Kim*

Abstract: AI applications often invoke a model before they determine whether inference is necessary. That default is useful for ambiguous generative tasks, but it is poorly matched to work that is structured, deterministic, policy-bound, lookup-oriented, or directly verifiable. KORA introduces deterministic-first execution control: a pre-inference control layer that represents requests as task paths, attempts deterministic or structured routes first, validates route outcomes, records telemetry, and escalates to a model-candidate path only when deterministic handling is insufficient. This paper presents KORA as a deterministic-first execution-control architecture and reports bounded evidence from reproducible benchmark and validation workloads. In a reproducible 100-task deterministic-heavy benchmark, KORA-controlled execution avoided 80 of 100 simulated model invocations versus a naive direct baseline, with zero mismatches in the deterministic subset. The workload is intentionally deterministic-heavy: it includes categories such as FAQ lookup, policy/rule checking, schema or format validation, deterministic transformation, and a smaller set of model-needed ambiguous or generative tasks. A separate synthetic customer-support local/no-network validation workload demonstrates route measurement and avoided model-call accounting in an application-shaped setting. These results support bounded model-invocation reduction and validation visibility in documented workloads. They do not establish production behavior, real-provider outcomes, energy outcomes, API-cost reduction, or broad superiority.

</details>

Abstract: Changing a deployment policy can invalidate a world model's uncertainty while leaving the physical transition mechanism and predictor unchanged. For one-step prediction, if source and target laws share the conditional mechanism $P(Y|X)=Q(Y|X)$, then the intervention is identified by the pre-outcome change of measure $w(X)=dQ_X/dP_X$. For policy-controlled dynamics this ratio factors into state-occupancy and action-propensity terms. Weighted conformal calibration transports marginal coverage to the target law; with an estimated ratio $\hat w$, $P_Q\{Y \in C_{\hat w}(X)\} \ge 1-α-TV(Q,\hat Q) \ge 1-α-E_P|w-\hat w|$. Population log-loss regret for a source-versus-target domain classifier bounds the same ratio error. If $h$ is its clipped target posterior, $ρ$ is the target-domain prior, and $Δ(h)$ is population log-loss regret, then $E_P|w-\hat w_h| \le (1/(ργ))\sqrt{Δ(h)/2}$. An exact cancellation improves the dependence from a generic odds-map bound of order $γ^{-2}$ to $(ργ)^{-1}$. Our experiments use a released DINO-feature world model and physical Franka trajectories. Under the strongest controlled state-action shift, ordinary 90% conformal sets cover 55.0%, whereas oracle and learned-ratio calibration cover 91.0% and 90.9%. The learned surrogate remains within 0.9% total variation of the target law. Calibration is relative to the deployment law: the model can remain fixed while calibration follows the intervention. We machine-check in Lean 4 the finite-support algebraic and causal identities used in the classifier-regret argument, including its coverage-composition step.

</details>

Abstract: Autonomous vertical landing of reusable launch vehicles is a critical yet formidable challenge in aerospace engineering, primarily due to highly nonlinear dynamics, stringent precision require- ments, and inherent environmental uncertainties. While reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a promising alternative to traditional model-based control, existing approaches frequently rely on flat training paradigms and highly simplified initial conditions, drastically limiting their real-world applicability. This paper proposes a comprehensive curriculum-driven deep reinforcement learning framework designed to master both far-range navigation and precision landing. By employing a three-stage curriculum augmented with a critic warm-up procedure and a rehearsal mechanism to prevent catastrophic forgetting, alongside a high-dimensional observation vector utilizing a full 3 X 3 rotation-matrix, the agent is trained to navigate from an initial horizontal distance of 1,000 to 1,500 units vastly farther than prior benchmarks before executing a soft touchdown. Crucially, we intro- duce a redesigned reward function that reduces unnecessary computational overhead and addresses limitations of earlier formulations, including poorly calibrated reward components and potentially conflicting optimization objectives. The redesigned function bounds the per-step reward magnitude by (O(1)), while its progress term accumulates the total distance closed over the trajectory. The available 100,000-episode results report a 99.98% success rate for the proposed policy and 68.31% for the baseline under the evaluation conditions. In the evaluation, the proposed policy retained 69.1% of its initial fuel capacity and achieved a mean final distance from the pad centre of 7.07 units, compared with 13.10 units for the baseline. Landing visualizations are available in Video 1 and Video 2.

</details>

Abstract: Low-bit weight--activation quantization reduces the memory and computation needed to deploy large language models, but activation rounding can still degrade accuracy after calibration. Reconstruction from rounded inputs cannot distinguish activations within the same quantization bin. We propose ResCom, a low-rank correction that uses the activation rounding residual to compensate for the resulting layer-output error. An output-aware calibration procedure selects residual directions by their contribution to output reconstruction and computes the correction in closed form, without gradient training or updates to the calibrated weights. On Llama-3-8B with 4-bit weights and activations, rank-32 attention corrections reduce WikiText-2 perplexity from 7.708 to 7.147, improve MMLU by 2.89 percentage points, and raise five-shot GSM8K accuracy from 23.96 to 36.16 percent. Evaluations on models from 1B to 70B parameters and on held-out C4 test the method beyond this setting. The evaluated FP32 implementation adds 2.25 percent to 4K prefill time; a separate compact-storage implementation retains a 27.7 percent peak-memory reduction relative to FP16.

</details>

Abstract: Distributed speculative decoding has emerged as a promising approach for device--server collaborative large language model (LLM) inference, but its decoding speedup is often limited by the vocabulary-scale communication overhead. In existing distributed speculative decoding methods, exact rejection sampling requires transmitting full token distributions over the LLM vocabulary between the device and the server, which becomes costly in bandwidth-constrained environments. To address this issue, we propose DISCO, a communication-efficient framework for distributed speculative decoding, that eliminates vocabulary-scale distribution transfer while preserving the exact target distribution. DISCO achieves this by introducing a server-side replica of the draft small language model (SLM), which enables the server to locally obtain the draft-side distribution required for rejection sampling. As a result, DISCO exchanges only drafted token indices and scalar probability values between the device and the server. Experimental results on multiple datasets show that DISCO consistently achieves higher decoding speedup than existing distributed speculative decoding methods, particularly under stringent bandwidth constraints.

</details>

Abstract: When events resume after a long silence, explicit Gaussian point-process filters amplify the first update exponentially in the silence duration. We give the exact distribution of this update and a computable index that predicts when it overshoots. For the explicit (single-scoring-step) filter and its implicit posterior-mode counterpart, we derive second-order silence expansions of the state variance with opposite-sign logarithmic corrections. The crossing index determines when the post-wake prediction exceeds the resumed rate, separating the bin width at which the amplification exponent reaches one from the larger width at which the true rate is overshot. At fixed bin width, the implicit filter's expected loss grows logarithmically with silence duration, whereas a continuous-time counterexample rules out a bound uniform in bin width and silence duration. Experimental evaluation confirms these predictions in all 73 crossing cells and on exchange trade data, while variance caps reduce explicit overshoot but mostly hurt the implicit filter.

</details>

  Methods: A retrospectively prepared LIDC-IDRI cohort contained 2,654 nodules labeled benign, indeterminate, or malignant using median radiologist ratings. Patient-disjoint splits comprised 1,876 training, 382 validation, and 396 test nodules. Representations included one axial slice, five neighboring slices, and a local CT volume. Each track evaluated a randomly initialized baseline, momentum-contrast pretraining followed by fine-tuning, and supervised training with mild augmentation. Checkpoints were selected by validation Macro-F1. The 2D experiments used three seeds; 2.5D and 3D results were single-run estimates. A separate binary experiment excluded indeterminate nodules.

Binary Macro-F1 values were $0.8227$, $0.8664$, and $0.7554$, respectively.
  Conclusion: Augmented 2.5D achieved the highest observed performance. Differences in preprocessing, optimization, capacity, and replication limit attribution to dimensionality. Labels reflect radiologist-assessed malignancy risk rather than pathology-confirmed cancer. Code and experimental results are available at https://github.com/CT-DeepLearning-Team/CT-2D-2.5D-3D-DeepLearning.

Abstract: Diabetic retinopathy grading from retinal fundus photographs is important but difficult to deploy on resource-constrained hardware. We conducted a controlled study of offline response-based knowledge distillation for five-class diabetic retinopathy grading on APTOS 2019, comparing ResNet-50, MobileNetV3-Large, and Swin Transformer-Tiny (Swin-T) teachers distilled into a MobileNetV3-Small student. A 3 x 4 x 2 grid of teacher, temperature, and hard-label weight was screened at one seed, while six configurations and the no-distillation baseline were evaluated across three seeds. The highest observed single-seed quadratic weighted kappa was 0.9093 (macro-F1 0.6851) for Swin-T at temperature 4 and hard-label weight 0.7; the corresponding three-seed mean was 0.9024, compared with 0.8804 for the baseline. The student used 1.52 M parameters and 5.94 MB, representing an 18.07x parameter reduction relative to Swin-T. Temperature and hard-label weight effects were teacher- and configuration-dependent, while small performance differences were sensitive to random seed. Knowledge distillation produced a compact student with competitive performance under the evaluated protocol, but independent external validation remains necessary.

Abstract: While progress in informal mathematics has come largely from scaling the chain-of-thought and agentic reasoning of frontier language models, formal theorem proving has increasingly relied on human-designed workflows around these models, such as planning, decomposition, and orchestrating specialized provers. This raises a bitter-lesson question: can a simple agentic loop that scales test-time compute match these workflows? The answer matters as formal verification must scale to keep up with frontier models producing research mathematics, yet new workflows rarely settle it, as their gains are often confounded with stronger models or more compute. We study this question with h1, a simple harness that gives a model an efficient interface to Lean 4 and keeps it working through verifier feedback and self-reflection, without imposing a decomposition or search structure. h1 reaches state-of-the-art results on PutnamBench, FATE-X, and FormalConjectures100-Solved, settles eight open problems in FormalConjectures, and, at matched models, matches or outperforms specialized workflows such as Goedel-Architect and AlphaProof Nexus, surprisingly showing that a single attempt (pass@1) can often scale to solve open problems. Through ablations and matched-model comparisons with Claude Code and AxProverBase, we find that simplicity alone is not enough: an efficient Lean interface largely determines cost and solve rate. We thus recommend that future work evaluate frontier models with efficient execution feedback before prescribing workflows, at the risk of bitter lessons.

</details>

Abstract: Generative machine learning provides an end-to-end framework for aerodynamic design optimization by generating diverse aerodynamic configurations conditioned on target performance. However, the generated designs may violate geometric constraints or deviate from the prescribed aerodynamic performance. Therefore, we propose a Condition-Enhanced Generative Adversarial Network (CEGAN) that embeds geometric and aerodynamic information into the generative process. A condition-informed loss function is developed by combining a geometry-guided feasibility penalty with an aerodynamic performance penalty evaluated by surrogate models trained on the same dataset. Additionally, an output filtering mechanism discards infeasible designs using these surrogate models, ensuring compliance with prescribed conditions. The method is demonstrated on aircraft configurations similar to the Cessna Citation X. Each configuration is represented by 28 shape parameters, whose aerodynamic performance is evaluated using OpenVSP. Interpolation and extrapolation tests are conducted for different network architectures and conditioning strategies. Compared with the baseline model, CEGAN reduces the average generation error by at least 36% for interpolated conditions and by at least 46% under extreme extrapolation conditions, while successfully producing diverse and physically feasible designs that extend beyond the training distribution. Therefore, the proposed CEGAN framework offers a robust and accurate tool for rapid aerodynamic configuration exploration during the conceptual design phase.

</details>

Abstract: We present LatentLieODE, a novel neural ODE method to discover linear Lie symmetries and first integrals of the learned dynamics from timeseries observations such as video inputs. It starts with an autoencoder, which learns a low dimensional representation of the observed system and then maps trajectories to decorrelated latent coordinates $z \in \mathbb{R}^d$. A bank of linear infinitesimal generators $\mathbf{A}_k \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d}$ is fitted jointly against a differentiable Lie-bracket residual $\mathbf{J}_f(z)\mathbf{A}z - \mathbf{A} f(z)$, while an MLP optimises the latent ODE $f(z) = \mathrm{d}z/\mathrm{d}t$ to reconstruct the observation-space timeseries. After suitable generators and $f$ are found, we fit a third network $H_φ$ to generate the first integral of the system. Across six benchmark systems (harmonic oscillator, simple and twin pendulums, Lotka-Volterra, pixel pendulum, sphere geodesics) we find each system's relevant symmetries, and the discovered invariants match ground-truth conserved quantities at $|ρ| \geq 0.978$. On the sphere, the pipeline recovers a non-abelian $\mathfrak{so}(3)$, certified by the negative-definite Killing form of its recovered structure constants. This work presents significant advances in data-driven discovery of symmetries and provides a new pathway for neural surrogates of dynamical systems.

</details>

Abstract: Leak localization in water distribution networks is challenging when multiple leaks occur simultaneously, particularly under sparse monitoring. Existing data-driven methods often assume a single or predefined number of leaks or require extensive enumeration of leak combinations. This study proposes a graph neural network (GNN) model that localizes a variable number of simultaneous leaks without specifying the leak count in advance or exhaustively generating every possible combination of leak locations for training. The model is evaluated on two benchmark networks of different sizes, the 31-node Hanoi network and the larger 92-node NET3 network, under variable demand, sparse pressure-sensor coverage, variable leak magnitudes, and varying numbers of simultaneous leaks. The GNN outperforms multilayer perceptron, support vector machine, K-nearest neighbors, and random forest classifiers by up to 12% and 20% in accuracy respectively in Hanoi and NET3 networks. Consistent performance across both networks supports the scalability of the proposed approach.

</details>

Abstract: Wind farm layout optimization becomes increasingly difficult as turbine count grows, especially when high-fidelity aerodynamic models are used. Conventional Kriging-based Bayesian optimization often suffers from low sample efficiency in high-dimensional layout spaces because sparse training data provide limited domain coverage. We develop a Graph-informed Kriging framework that embeds reusable aerodynamic interaction information into the surrogate mean function. The wind farm is represented as a directed aerodynamic graph, where pairwise turbine interactions defined in a low-dimensional relative-coordinate space are assembled into a farm-scale mean. This structured mean captures the dominant variation in annual energy production (AEP), allowing the Gaussian process to model only the residual structure and uncertainty. Numerical experiments show that Graph-informed Kriging consistently improves AEP prediction accuracy over ordinary and generic-trend Kriging under sparse-data conditions, with increasing benefits as layout dimension and wake-interaction strength grow. Embedded in Bayesian optimization, it substantially improves sample efficiency and achieves competitive solutions for the IEA Wind Task 37 benchmark with about 280 AEP evaluations. In a controlled 16-turbine case, it also outperforms ordinary and multi-fidelity Kriging under the same high-fidelity evaluation budget. The framework is further extended to a 48-turbine wind farm, demonstrating applicability to large-scale CFD-based layout optimization. These results show that Graph-informed surrogate modeling can enable more efficient use of high-fidelity aerodynamic models for reliable large-scale wind farm layout design.

</details>

Abstract: Conditional memory layers, such as DeepSeek's Engram, attach large hashed n-gram tables to a language model, and removing them from DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash costs 36.8 PopQA points. Low-bit quantization loses long-tail facts first, and the standard remedy, giving more bits to frequently accessed rows, backfires: refining lookups by access frequency is worse than refining them at random. We trace this to the gate that scales each retrieved row. A lookup's quantization error is weighted by its gate, which is small for frequent n-grams, large for the rare n-grams of long-tail entities, and computable from a 1-bit code. GateRQ therefore stores each row as a ladder of 1-bit residual codes, keeps level 1 in GPU memory to compute the gate, and reads deeper levels only for the lookups the gate ranks highest. Without retraining, GateRQ cuts GPU table memory by 7.8x on DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, which raises vLLM throughput by 11%-31% over FP8 tables, while losing about 1 PopQA point at 1.50 bits read per lookup and 0.2 points at 2.10 bits across two models.

</details>

Abstract: Variable-length training creates conflicting parallelism requirements across the layers of a hybrid-attention model. The longest samples require wide sequence-parallel (SP) groups of 16 or 32 ranks for full attention, whose cost grows quadratically with sequence length. Sparse-attention layers, dominated by near-linear MoE work, can instead balance most evaluated workloads within node-local SP8 groups. Reusing the wide groups for these layers pushes their all-to-all communication across nodes, where the observed effective rate is 7.0x lower. Yet switching to smaller groups changes which ranks own each sample; a separate redistribution at every full-sparse boundary would introduce another data transfer.
  Layerwise Repacking keeps each SP16/32 parent for full attention and repacks its samples into node-local sparse children at SP{1, 2, 4, 8}, where hidden states persist between full layers. The layout change rides on the two existing full-layer Ulysses sites: at each, one variable-size all-to-all performs both the head exchange and the sample repacking, so no redistribution pass is added. Because the balance the children can reach depends on which samples share a parent, an auxiliary parent solver rebalances parent membership before the children are formed.
  On all 22 steps with SP16/32 parents among the first 200 training steps of a 1F3S hybrid-attention model (one full-attention layer per three sparse-attention layers), repacking alone reduces the mean end-to-end time by 5.95% and raises model FLOPs utilization (MFU) by 3.75 percentage points over the baseline with repacking disabled; with the improved solver, end-to-end time falls by 7.61% and MFU rises by 4.83 percentage points.

</details>

Abstract: Sparse expert execution does not remove the stored states needed to adapt a mixture-of-experts language model. We present DuplexTune, a framework for replaying coupled hidden states through pretrained attention and expert modules. Trainable width bridges connect two residual streams to the backbone. Bridge warm-up precedes backbone adaptation, with router weights kept frozen. We analyze the implicit state-recovery equation and identify the convergence and replay conditions needed for reconstruction. On Qwen1.5-MoE-A2.7B, the single-H800 evaluation reports 38.8 GB peak memory, versus 64.8 GB for supervised fine-tuning with checkpointing, and throughput of 25.1 versus 19.9 samples/s. DuplexTune achieves 67.2% MMLU, 75.6% GSM8K, and 7.69 MT-Bench, with a weaker multilingual score than the checkpointed baseline. The results characterize a configuration-dependent tradeoff among state storage, throughput, and adaptation quality.

</details>

Abstract: Dynamic creative optimization decides which creative of an ad to render for each request. Recent work places that choice before ad ranking, but relies on deterministic point predictors with no calibrated uncertainty. AI-generated content has made creatives nearly free, while feedback per creative remains sparse and selection faces a tight latency budget. We introduce a creative pre-ranker that scores every recalled creative and shortlists each campaign's pool before the per-user ranker runs. Creatives sit in a hierarchy of audience segment, advertised item, campaign and creative, so a cold creative falls back on what its parent levels have earned, and how much it borrows from its parents is set in closed form from historical logs, with one parameter per level and nothing tuned. To stay within the latency budget, the pre-ranker predicts for an audience segment rather than an individual user, from per-attribute counters. It selects by a single Thompson draw per creative, balancing exploration and exploitation so that a new creative still has a chance to be learned. Replaying Roblox Sponsored-ads traffic over campaigns with competing creatives, the shortlist lifts predicted play-through rate by 8.14% against 2.80% for an equally sized random shortlist, forwards more than 40% fewer creatives than the full pool, and adds only 0.2% to serving time. After an A/B test on live traffic, the method now serves in production at Roblox.

</details>

Abstract: The information bottleneck (IB) trades compression of an input $X$ against prediction of a target $Y$ through a multiplier $β=1/λ$, yet each operating point is usually served by training, or fully conditioning, a new encoder. For jointly Gaussian data, we show that the IB solution factorizes as $A(β)=D(β)W$: the information-bearing directions $W$ do not depend on $β$, and $β$ acts only through scalar gains. We ask whether this separation of \emph{representation} from \emph{rate control} survives beyond Gaussian models, treating the budget as a given operating parameter rather than a quantity to be optimized. For a budget-free representation \(F(X)\), the \emph{separation gap} is controlled by the \emph{sufficiency deficit} $ε_F=I(X;Y\mid F(X))$: We show there is at least $ε_F$ in the worst case for finite inputs, and it is bounded uniformly in $λ$ by a function of $ε_F$ for finite label alphabets. The same certificate extends to a new target distribution and to pooled multi-domain data, where a conditional-alignment term appears. Abstract: Hardware-accelerated reinforcement learning in JAX has gained rapid popularity in recent years and has proven to be a successful method of scaling online reinforcement learning. The resulting ecosystem, however, is fragmented: environment suites define their own interfaces, and algorithm implementations are typically written for a single suite, a single type of space, and either JAX-native or host-based environments. Here we present Jaxnasium, a library for JAX-based reinforcement learning, aiming to make the field more accessible. Jaxnasium represents observation and action spaces as PyTrees, and uses this structure to build networks and support multi-agent processing. Through this, its near-single-file implementations run on discrete, continuous, image, and composite spaces, as well as in multi-agent environments, without any changes to the algorithm code. The same files serve as readable, research-friendly algorithms, as well as importable algorithms for more applied workloads. Through a minimal Gymnasium-style interface and wrappers for 11 suites, over 300 environments are available on which a small PPO implementation with default hyperparameters is able to run. Additionally, through a simple wrapper, we can run Gymnasium and EnvPool environments with the same algorithm and training loop, with no performance loss compared to architectures specifically built for training JAX algorithms on host-based environments. Jaxnasium further provides tools for multi-seed evaluation, hyperparameter sweeps, and project scaffolding. Jaxnasium is open-sourced at https://github.com/ponseko/jaxnasium

Abstract: Temporal Predictive Coding (tPC) networks offer a layer-local alternative to backpropagation. For linear tPC, we show, by applying tools from cellular sheaf theory, that temporal credit assignment is a tug-of-war between spatial displacement and temporal errors, whose ratios are modulated by persistence and exposure operators $P$ and $Q$. Spatial inference therefore gives us the effective recurrent transition $F=PA$, whilst future credit is filtered by $P^\ast$. This creates a persistence--error tradeoff in spatial modes: under non-amplifying invariant recurrence, modes that preserve influence over long delays simultaneously fail to expose that influence strongly as temporal error. Recurrence can circumvent this via routing between persistent and exposed modes.

Abstract: Financial institutions operating under data-residency constraints are restricted to open-weight models small enough to locally host, which trail frontier systems on multi-step quantitative reasoning. On-policy distillation narrows such gaps by supervising a student on its own rollouts, but traditional OPD requires a strong external teacher, and on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) conditions on a full reference solution. We ask how much of that signal is necessary. We present Fin-SD, a localized hindsight self-distillation recipe for multi-step reasoning. Given a problem the student fails, the student itself identifies the first incorrect reasoning step and writes a short critique of that step. We truncate the student's trajectory immediately before the error (aiming to obtain a correct prefix), condition a frozen copy of the student on the same prefix plus the hint, and distill that hint-conditioned teacher into the student over a short rollout under reverse KL. No external model contributes a gradient. We apply Fin-SD to GPT-OSS-20B on a custom multi-step financial reasoning benchmark that requires deriving formula inputs from realistic scenarios via accounting conventions. We find: (i) replacing the critique with a generic-feedback control matches Fin-SD's entire 3.03 pp accuracy gain over the base model on the FinanceReasoning Hard subset, suggesting the gain comes from where supervision is applied, not hint content; (ii) the model writing the hint does not need to be stronger than the model receiving it: hints from same-family GPT-OSS-120B, at roughly 6x the parameters, yield no materially greater benefits than self-written hints; and (iii) Fin-SD matches on-policy self-distillation from full reference solutions on accuracy while generating 9.5% fewer tokens. With Fin-SD, general reasoning on MMLU-Pro and a fourteen-metric compliance audit on FinTrust show no material regression.

</details>

Abstract: The quality with which language models follow instructions falls as the load grows, yet there is still no quantity that expresses the limit of a model in units of load with an uncertainty. We propose an instrument in which the load consists of rules that replace single-token words in a text without meaning: such an instruction is atomic, the rules are independent by the construction of the task, and the outcome of every application of a rule is determined by a computable reference answer. Every occurrence of a word to be replaced becomes a Bernoulli trial with a known position in the answer and a known rule number. The statistical model first measures the shape of the dependence of the probability of a right replacement on the text length, the word position, the replacement density, the number of rules and the rule number, then describes it by smooth laws and restores a reference level that does not depend on the choice of the coding. The model yields the instruction capacity - the number of rules held at a given share of right replacements - with an uncertainty. A measurement of GPT-4o mini (2958 requests, 642,430 trials) gave regular laws for every load factor and for the collapse of the answer; the instruction capacity was about 37-38 rules at a share of right replacements of 0.95, and about 18-19 rules at the full replacement density. The code of the instrument, the measurement data and the passport are open, and every number of the passport is recomputed from the published points. The passport of a model serves as the calibration of a future meter of the instruction load of prompts.

</details>

Abstract: We study full-information online linear optimisation on the unit $\ell_p$ ball in $\mathbb{R}^d$, for fixed $2\le p<\infty$ and losses of dual norm at most one. Let $Δ_T$ denote the cumulative loss of the best fixed action above each round's minimum. A follow-the-regularised-leader (FTRL) algorithm attains, on every loss sequence and without knowing $T$ or $Δ_T$, regret $O_p(1+\min\{Δ_T^{1-1/p},\, d^{1/2-1/p}\sqrt{Δ_T}\})$. Matching lower bounds show that this rate is minimax for each fixed $p$, and its constant $(2+o(1))p/\log p$ is optimal as $p\to\infty$ among guarantees uniform in $d$ and $T$. The algorithm uses a bounded radial regulariser and switches at most once to its quadratic augmentation. On some paths where follow-the-leader (FTL) has regret one, its regret grows with $T$. A switching extension keeps the sharp constant and also guarantees $\mathrm{Reg}_T\le 2\,\mathrm{Reg}_T^{\mathrm{FTL}}$. For i.i.d. losses whose mean has no zero coordinates, we compute the exact $\log T$ coefficient of the expected regret, which separates FTL's variance term from the bias of the regulariser.

Abstract: Test-time updating lets a language model change persistent state after training---such as model weights, adapter parameters, or a memory module---before or while answering queries. Existing evaluations often compare systems using update count or frequency and performance measured immediately afterward. These summaries omit what is updated, where, when, and in what order. The same total can therefore hide an important difference: one system may answer after one update while another answers after two. We introduce the Control Map, an evaluation framework that records the committed updates preceding each scored answer and separately evaluates behavioral changes, immediate learning (acquisition), later recall (retention), preservation of unrelated behavior (locality), and performance in new settings. We prove when counts alone determine final state and show, under stated assumptions, how differences between pre-answer histories limit differences in average scores. Using ControlMap, our experiments reveal three insights. First, update scheduling matters: at a 760M-parameter checkpoint, 24 schedules with the same update count yield 19.7%--34.2% accuracy. Second, learning an edit does not guarantee that it lasts or remains contained: all six selected CounterFact LoRA settings learn the new information, while ROME retains edits at all 13 operating points but fails locality at each. Third, no editor performs best consistently across models, benchmarks, and tests. Evaluations should therefore report update histories and assess acquisition, retention, and locality separately.

</details>

These findings support representation-consistent neural adaptation and joint physiological modeling across stroke, healthy-sleep, and sleep-disorder cohorts.

Abstract: Semiparametric Bayesian networks (SPBNs) are flexible probabilistic graphical models for representing joint distributions of continuous variables through lower-dimensional conditional probability distributions (CPDs). Existing SPBN models use kernel density estimators (KDEs) for non-parametric CPD modeling, but their one-component-per-observation mixture representation can lead to increased inference costs and density estimation errors, which can be limiting. In this work, we define a new CPD modeling approach for non-parametric components based on Bayesian non-parametric Dirichlet Process Mixtures (DPMs) within SPBNs. DPMs provide a data-adaptive clustering of observations, yielding a more compact representation while retaining the flexibility of non-parametric density estimation, unlike KDE. This results in lower inference costs while improving density estimation performance. We implement the proposed DPM-based SPBN (DPM-SPBN) by extending the open-source PyBNesian package and evaluate it on synthetic and real-world data. For a given network structure, DPM-based CPDs significantly outperform state-of-the-art Bayesian network models for continuous variables in density estimation, achieving lower absolute log-likelihood errors on synthetic data. We further investigate the impact of the proposed CPD modeling on structure learning methods relying on CPD-based scores. DPM-based SPBNs achieve lower structure learning and density estimation errors than KDE-based SPBNs. Experiments on real-world data corroborate these findings, identifying DPM-based CPD modeling as the most effective alternative among the evaluated non-parametric approaches for continuous variables.

</details>

Abstract: The normalized cut (NCut) problem is a fundamental one in the area of data clustering. Despite its fractional structure, the NCut problem is seldom considered from a fractional programming (FP) perspective in the existing literature. The main reason is that the conventional FP method is inadequate for this problem case: the classical Dinkelbach's transform can only handle a single ratio and hence is limited to the two-class clustering, whereas the multi-class clustering involves multiple ratio terms. This work proposes a novel so-called multidimensional quadratic transform that is capable of dealing with the multi-ratio case; it recasts the NCut problem into a bipartite matching problem---which can be immediately solved by linear search. Furthermore, we explore the connection between the proposed FP method and the minorization-maximization (MM) theory, and in turn characterize the convergence behavior of our algorithm. Finally, we conduct experiments to numerically demonstrate the advantages of the proposed FP-based algorithm in a variety of data clustering and image segmentation tasks.

</details>

Abstract: Grokking is a delayed transition from memorization to generalization in deep neural networks (NNs). We examine whether scaling and renormalization group (RG) theories can describe this dynamics, establishing a quantitative physical framework for neural generalization transitions. For modular addition on a two-layer NN, an ensemble of trained models defines an order parameter (the squared weight norm) and fluctuation proxies for the heat capacity and the susceptibility, with training epoch and weight decay serving as thermal and field control coordinates. To bridge neural architectures to real-space RG, we construct an exact coupling matrix via a second-order expansion of the loss with respect to per-neuron norms and use its Fiedler vector to define an effective geometry. This spectral ordering yields a monotonic spatial decay of coupling strength that is absent under random permutations. We then execute real-space RG by block-spin aggregating adjacent neurons and parameterizing the coupling matrix into off-diagonal band strengths. Linearizing this local RG map yields relevant scaling exponents and five derived critical exponents, with direct empirical estimates matching RG predictions within 1-10%. Overall, our framework provides a consistent effective description of grokking, suggesting the scaling and renormalization group formalism as a tool to investigate NN dynamics.

</details>

Abstract: On a chain of $N$ states, any local learning rule with bounded communication range needs $Θ(N)$ rounds before the loss can influence the earliest parameters, and this latency is usually attributed to depth. We show that it is a property of the communication geometry rather than of depth. For $N=m^d$ states placed at constant density in $d$ dimensions, a light-cone argument gives an $Ω(N^{1/d})$ lower bound on the number of bounded-range rounds that any local algorithm needs before its gradient estimates can be sign-correct at every parameter. We construct a unit-range, degree-$2d$ directed acyclic graph that contains a Hamiltonian computation of length $N-1$ and in which every state lies within directed distance $d(m-1)=Θ(N^{1/d})$ of the output, matching the lower bound. In the realizable-circuit model, depth and credit depth are therefore independent parameters: represented depth $Θ(N)$ and causal credit latency $Θ(N^{1/d})$ coexist, with a separation of order $N^{1-1/d}$. SpatialPC realizes this separation within predictive coding. Credit is propagated by adjoint prediction dynamics (APD), a local update whose $K$-round state on a settled acyclic graph is the truncated reverse-path sum $a^K=\sum_{k<K}(J^\top)^k b$, with error exactly $(J^\top)^K a^\star$. Graph geometry fixes when credit can first arrive at a state; the Jacobian-weighted path tail fixes how many further rounds accurate credit requires. A sparse two-dimensional Moore compiler retains the $N-1$ backbone with $\tfrac32N-2$ unit-range edges and has measured worst-node reachability $\sqrt N+2$ up to $N=65{,}536$, where a 65,535-edge backbone is reached in 258 local hops. With $T=2\sqrt N$ rounds, SpatialPC keeps finite-round task gradients aligned with settled gradients (cosine $0.93$ on MNIST and $0.97$ on Fashion-MNIST at $N=256$) and reaches $97.6\%$ and $88.1\%$ test accuracy after 20 epochs.

Abstract: Dynamical coarse-graining of neural networks gives macroscopic observables and enables training to be predicted, and controlled in a state space far smaller than the parameter space. In this paper, we first define the learning operator following the training law of neural networks and establish a descent theorem for the learning operator, which serves to determine whether the operator is sufficient to characterize the learning dynamics. Then the exact closure theorem and the dynamic coarse-graining theorem are presented in sequence. The three theorems established in this paper provide a systematic framework for constructing and assessing dynamical coarse-grained models of neural-network training. Finally, we demonstrate the applicability of the theorems with a finite-width nonlinear ReLU network. Its dynamic coarse-grained model for a fixed pattern is exactly derived and composed of five independent moments. The model exactly predict the training outputs and evolving learning operator. The framework may also guide the development of approximate coarse-grained models for more general neural networks when exact closure is unavailable.

</details>

Abstract: The Persona Selection Model (PSM) posits that post-training selects a persona the model learned during pre-training. Work on subliminal learning has shown that models can impart covert signals (like "love of owls") into semantically unrelated outputs like strings of numbers, and that students trained on these outputs inherit those traits. In this work I show that by evaluating the likelihood of such outputs under the base pre-trained model with an appropriate header, a method I call persona-header likelihood evaluation (PHLE), one can decode these subliminal signals and classify between them. For example, PHLE distinguishes number sequences generated by a post-trained model prompted with "You love owls" from those prompted with "You love eagles", and distinguishes answers from a model with a secret desire to harm the user, filtered to remove any noticeable misalignment, from those of a model with no system prompt. In both cases prompted classifiers, including the generating model itself, remain near chance. The signal partially persists across model families, with the Qwen base model reading signals generated by Olmo and vice versa. This is evidence for a non-trivial prediction of the PSM, and the method may be practically useful for auditing the hidden objectives of models.

Abstract: Link prediction remains difficult in sparse graphs, where diffusion heuristics lack connectivity and geometric methods suffer spectral instability when coupling learned representations with diffusion operators. We introduce Concorde, an energy-based framework that resolves this by decomposing link likelihood into three structurally decoupled branches: (i) a parameter-free spectral anchor that establishes permutation-invariant topological stability; (ii) a mixed-curvature residual on a learnable $\mathbb{H}^{d_h}_{c_h} \times \mathbb{S}^{d_s}_{c_s}$ product manifold, regularized by a feature-aware Ollivier-Ricci curvature proxy; and (iii) a continuous vector field on the tangent bundle of hyperbolic space for non-local structural alignment across topological gaps. All three guarantees hold at every deterministic horizon, and the algorithm knows neither the horizon, nor the gap, nor the environment type. Abstract: Offline-to-online reinforcement learning uses offline pretraining to accelerate subsequent online learning. While many methods assume the offline dataset remains available throughout fine-tuning, there are many settings where this is not the case. Prior work attributes failures in this setting primarily to critic recalibration under distribution shift. We identify a complementary actor-side failure: once the offline data disappears, continued behavioral cloning (BC) no longer preserves the offline behavior distribution and instead refits the behavioral model to the agent's limited, and nonstationary, online experience. We demonstrate that stopping online behavioral fitting while preserving the pretrained expressive prior substantially improves no-retention fine-tuning across implicit flow Q-learning (IFQL), flow Q-learning (FQL), and action-chunked RL (QC) on seven OGBench domains. We trace this failure to a behavioral feedback loop: continuing online behavioral cloning rapidly erases the pretrained prior, inducing representational collapse and action diversity loss that starves the critic of useful exploration. Freezing the prior breaks this cycle, preserving offline coverage while allowing value learning to drive continuous improvement.

</details>

As an initial solution, we propose SURE, integrating dual-importance estimation, selective parameter perturbation, and importance-guided recovery. Extensive experiments with existing unlearning methods adapted to SFL reveal substantial forgetting-utility trade-offs across model architectures, data distributions, and update modes. SURE achieves competitive agreement with mode-matched retraining at modest additional cost, while further analyses highlight the impact of split position and update constraints on unlearning behavior. These findings establish SFU as a distinct research problem and provide a foundation for unlearning in partitioned federated systems.

Abstract: Deep reinforcement learning (RL) algorithms have been observed to lose the ability to adaptively learn from new data, a phenomenon termed Loss of Plasticity (LoP). Abstract: Large-scale linear programs with millions of variables and constraints strain time and memory budgets. First-order methods such as the primal-dual hybrid gradient scale to this regime because they avoid matrix factorizations, but they require many iterations. Learned accelerators can reduce these iterations, yet fewer iterations do not guarantee a shorter solve. Learning inference also takes time, and running the accelerator longer trades solver time for inference time. Existing unrolled accelerators handle this trade-off poorly: each layer processes every variable and constraint, the predicted iterates still need solver refinement, and every instance runs the same layers. We therefore ask: What neural accelerator design achieves end-to-end speedups on large-scale linear programs across sizes and target accuracies? We introduce AHEAD, an accelerator that predicts step sizes for first-order linear programming solvers while keeping their update rules. Its weight-shared predictor reads compact solver statistics and rescales the primal and dual step sizes, so each warm-start iteration stays inexpensive and a short warm-start phase hands the solver a warm start. A learned handoff policy sets the number of warm-start iterations for each instance and target accuracy, after which the solver finishes under its own termination criterion. On packing linear programs with up to $10^7$ variables, AHEAD achieves $6.5$--$11.6\times$ end-to-end speedups over Gurobi's first-order solver from a cold start, including on instances of unseen sizes and distributions. The gains extend to PageRank linear programs and hold across difficulty levels and target accuracies.

</details>

Abstract: The goal of Quality-Diversity Reinforcement Learning (QD-RL) is to find a population of high-performing policies that solve a decision-making problem in diverse ways. Despite recent progress, QD-RL methods predominantly rely on evolutionary operators--including black-box optimizers, mutations, and crossovers--to facilitate exploration and induce diversity. Scaling these methods becomes increasingly costly as policies and behavior spaces grow larger. On standard continuous-control tasks, RePPO achieves a strong quality-diversity tradeoff compared with state-of-the-art QD-RL methods. Ablations further characterize this tradeoff by examining how each component affects individual quality and collective diversity. To assess RePPO's scalability, we extend our evaluation to learning from pixels and fine-tuning large language models. In pixel-based tasks, where large policies make most traditional QD-RL methods infeasible, RePPO maintains quality on par with standard PPO while achieving substantially higher diversity. Its successful application to language model fine-tuning further demonstrates, for the first time, that QD-RL methods can scale to this domain. Together, these results show that directly optimizing compact populations with RePPO provides a scalable approach to QD-RL across increasingly complex domains.

Existing public datasets for ML-based wing design have focused primarily on aerodynamic performance, with limited consideration of the multidisciplinary interactions that govern practical aircraft design. The wing planform geometry is parameterized by span, sweep, dihedral, tip chord, and tip twist, while the airfoil cross-sections are parameterized using the class-shape transformation method. The analysis is further parameterized by Mach number, angle of attack, altitude, and structural load factor. The utility of the dataset is demonstrated by training state-of-the-art ML models to predict aerodynamic surface quantities, integrated aerodynamic performance quantities, and the failure distribution of the wingbox. ML models achieve at most 0.0309 normalized root mean squared error (NRMSE) in the prediction of aerodynamic quantities and at most 0.1189 NRMSE in the prediction of failure distribution of the wingbox. By providing coupled aerodynamic and structural responses for a diverse set of transonic wings, this dataset establishes a benchmark for developing, evaluating, and comparing multi-physics ML models for wing design. Broadly, it is intended to encourage creation of similar high-fidelity multi-physics benchmark datasets across engineering domains.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents interact with their environments through executable harnesses that govern tool use and feedback. Abstract: Classical Langevin Monte Carlo algorithms, such as the unadjusted Langevin algorithm (ULA), are based on discretization schemes of the overdamped Langevin dynamics and are widely used for sampling in machine learning because they scale to high dimensions and large datasets. However, they require differentiable log-densities, and are less efficient for sampling heavy-tailed distributions. Although various approaches have been proposed in the literature for sampling non-differentiable and heavy-tailed targets, all of them are developed in the overdamped setting only. We propose a novel momentum-based underdamped anchored Langevin dynamics (UALD), and underdamped anchored Langevin Monte Carlo (UALMC) algorithm based on a discretization scheme of UALD. Our paper is the first to provide a kinetic (momentum-based) Langevin algorithm for non-differentiable and heavy-tailed targets that is free of asymptotic bias. The method replaces the original potential with a smooth reference potential and modifies the underdamped Langevin dynamics via multiplicative scaling in the drift term. We establish non-asymptotic guarantees in the 2-Wasserstein distance to the target distribution and show that the resulting iteration complexity has better dependence on dimension and accuracy level compared to overdamped-based anchored Langevin algorithms. We provide numerical experiments to illustrate the theory and practical performance of our proposed approach.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) improves language-model post-training by letting the student learn from its own sampled trajectories under teacher supervision, aligning the training process with inference-time behavior. However, the rollout policy in OPD is usually treated as fixed: each round samples directly from the current student. We show that this choice can create a compounding teacher--student mismatch, because it requires the teacher to provide effective supervision on whichever trajectories the student samples, including ones less reachable by the teacher itself. On such trajectories, teacher supervision can become noisy or even misleading, leading to undesirable student patterns such as repetition. Moreover, since the updated student in turn defines the rollout policy of subsequent rounds, these patterns persist and potentially amplify over training. This failure mode suggests that improving OPD requires treating the rollout policy itself as an optimization target, rather than only modifying the distillation objective. We propose Mixture Proposal On-Policy Distillation (MPOPD), which derives a student-anchored, teacher-guided rollout distribution from a local trust-region objective based on relative teacher support. The resulting target is a geometric mixture of the student and teacher policies, and we introduce a proposal policy that approximates this mixture, enabling rollout generation in a single forward pass. Experiments on 16 math, knowledge, and medical reasoning benchmarks show consistent gains over OPD and recent variants. On math reasoning, our method improves average accuracy by 6.91 over OPD on Qwen3-4B-Base while reducing average generation length by 41%, suggesting that rollout policy design provides an effective way to improve OPD.

</details>

PRISM evaluates eight foundation models (CLIP, PLIP, CONCH, UNI, Virchow2, GigaPath, H-Optimus-0, Midnight) on six tile-level datasets at six label fractions (1% to 100%) with three seeds (864 linear probe runs), extended with a five-hospital Camelyon17 study of 20 directed transfer pairs. Four phenomena emerge. (i) Under full supervision, AUROC rank predicts post-temperature calibration rank consistently across datasets (Spearman rho = 0.52, I^2 = 0%), whereas at 1% labels the relationship becomes dataset-specific (rho = -0.24, I^2 = 67%). (ii) On harder datasets the linear probe collapses to a single predicted class below a dataset-specific label fraction. (iii) Whether additional source-domain labels help or harm target calibration tracks how well the probe transfers: below a transfer AUROC of roughly 0.85, more labels worsen target ECE in 86-88% of combinations. (iv) Temperature scaling fails to reduce ECE in 49 of 288 cells, miscalibration is predominantly under-confidence, and a source-fitted temperature is harmful under failing transfer. PRISM is released as a pip-installable package with reference results for all 288 cells. Code and data: https://github.com/alicemkyn/prism-benchmark

Abstract: Derivative-free optimization (DFO) addresses problems where derivatives are unavailable or costly to obtain. This setting often arises in simulation-based and experimental applications, where evaluating the black-box objective can itself be costly. Thus, it remains a central challenge in DFO to effectively exploit accumulated observations to select informative evaluation points under a limited budget. In this light, we introduce the NextEval solver, an agent-based DFO solver, which integrates the capabilities of a large language model (LLM) to guide the sequential selection of evaluation points. Specifically, we establish a system that combines an LLM agent with a numerical environment. The environment provides tools for analyzing past evaluations and executing numerical methods, while maintaining a shared evaluation history and method-specific state to support continuation across method switches. Using observations and tool feedback, the LLM agent selects tools, configures their parameters, and allocates the evaluation budget, while numerical methods compute and evaluate candidate points. Building on this system, we also introduce NextEval Bench, a benchmark protocol for comparing agent configurations on shared DFO tasks using the same numerical tools under matched evaluation budgets and information constraints. Numerical results on low-dimensional DFO problems demonstrate that the NextEval solver outperforms the state-of-the-art DFO baselines in function-evaluation efficiency.

Abstract: Large language models have recently shown strong potential for automatically optimizing GPU kernels, yet their effectiveness depends critically on the hardware information available to the kernel optimization model. Existing systems typically rely on predefined profiling strategies, leaving what to profile largely outside the optimization process. We show that effective profiling is not simply about collecting more information: increasing the number of profiling metrics does not monotonically improve kernel optimization, while the most useful profiling information varies across kernels. Our theoretical analysis further shows that, under such heterogeneous profiling utility, kernel-conditioned selection can outperform any single fixed profiling configuration. Motivated by this observation, we propose AutoProfiler, a feedback-driven framework for learning a deterministic profiling tree from optimization feedback. The tree is iteratively refined through REWRITE and SPLIT operations, with an LLM generating candidate modifications and empirical evaluation determining their acceptance. Once constructed, the tree quickly gathers the required profiling information for any kernel, allowing the LLM rewriter to be invoked only once. Experiments on KernelBench show that our approach improves kernel optimization over fixed profiling configurations under constrained profiling budgets. These results suggest that hardware profiling should evolve from static metric collection toward profiling reasoning, making profiling itself a new dimension for improving kernel optimization. The codes are available at https://github.com/Satissss/AutoProfiler.

</details>

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) are being applied to Web data streams such as network intrusion detection, where they adapt by updating the labelled rows of their context without retraining. When a past concept returns after leaving the context, a TFM faces a recall-or-relearn decision between restoring stored rows of the concept and relearning it from new labels. We propose MICE (Mixture of In-Context Experts), a training-free learner that cuts the stream into segments at detected changes. It merges a closed segment into a stored context when the TFM's estimated additional error on the segment is within a tolerance, under a capacity limit on stored contexts. A fast level weights the stored contexts and first-in-first-out (FIFO) windows by the log-loss of their predictions on the last labelled batch. We show that, with pure stored contexts and a margin condition, this fast level gains over relearning, in expected accuracy, the normalised positive area between the stored context's accuracy and the TFM's learning curve, up to a first-batch term. A slow level mixes the fast level's prediction with that of the FIFO window of the last labelled rows and bounds the discounted half-Brier regret of MICE against this window at every time. Learning curves measured beforehand predict the ordering of the observed recall gains. MICE outperforms reset, which empties the context at detected drifts, on grid streams with random boundary phases. Merging keeps fewer experts than pools that store every segment, and with fixed segmentation a time-matched snapshot pool matches MICE on the shifted grid. On real streams, MICE has the highest mean accuracy of all policies we compare.

Abstract: Low-precision training often relies on stochastic rounding (SR) to keep quantized gradients unbiased and prevent small updates from rounding to zero. Standard SR draws an independent random threshold for every gradient element. However, accelerators generate random numbers on vector units whose throughput lags far behind their matrix units. Drawing one threshold per element saturates the vector unit and can make INT8 training with SR slower than BF16 training. Sharing random thresholds across tensor elements removes this cost but forces those elements to round in the same direction and shifts their rounding errors by a common offset. We find that this offset degrades training primarily through the activation gradient, which propagates the error backward into earlier layers. Because earlier layers cannot cancel an incoming offset using their own local rounding errors, the offset must be removed at the layer where it originates. We propose Structured Subspace Stochastic Rounding (S3R), which quantizes each upstream gradient once for both the activation and weight gradient matrix multiplications and shares a single threshold per token across the hidden dimension. Its error correction, S3R-EC, subtracts the structured mean rounding error from the activation and weight gradients while leaving the optimizer unchanged. In LLM pre-training experiments, uncorrected threshold sharing degrades convergence, whereas S3R-EC eliminates this degradation and matches the validation loss of independent SR. Furthermore, S3R-EC accelerates full INT8 training steps of dense LLMs on TPU v5p by 1.24x to 1.32x over BF16 and by 1.11x over independent SR.

</details>

Abstract: Mamba-2 compresses all context into a fixed-size recurrent state that dominates decoding memory and bandwidth. Existing compressors quantize state values or prune state coordinates, scoring each channel independently in the native basis. We examine trained Mamba-2 states and find that energy is almost rank-one, but the output does not read the energetic directions: energy concentrates in a bias-like direction accumulated by the recurrence, while the content the output reads lives in a low-dimensional subspace that is not aligned with the state's coordinates. Even output-aware scores are computed coordinate by coordinate, so they miss the directions that determine the input--output map. Compression should therefore target the write--read subspace rather than individual coordinates. We propose \method{}, a post-training transform that instantiates balanced truncation with full controllability and observability Gramians and applies the rotation exactly via Mamba-2's scalar-identity transition. Because the useful rank varies widely across layers, we further introduce HARP, which allocates ranks per layer from the Hankel spectrum and protects retrieval-sensitive layers, improving both perplexity and retrieval over uniform budgets. Calibration takes a single forward pass, an order of magnitude faster than GHOST's released protocol, with lower peak memory. With HARP, \method{} halves the state dimension at an average WikiText-2 perplexity increase of $0.3$ points ($1.8\%$) across 130M--2.7B, versus $1.6$ ($9.3\%$) for GHOST, and the gap widens at 25\% retention.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) enable algorithm discovery for combinatorial optimization problems (COPs) through iterative program generation and evaluation. A central challenge is how to organize search to discover better algorithms within a limited budget. Our key insight is that past search trajectories and their recorded outcomes form an experience world, which can be used to facilitate the COP solving through replay. We introduce Dream-COP, in which a \emph{policy agent} learns executable policies through \emph{dreaming} in this experience world to guide a \emph{discovery agent} in generating and refining COP programs. Specifically, policy replay provides feedback on the quality of the programs revealed and the search steps used, to guide policy selection more effectively. Across five COPs, \textsc{Dream} discovers better algorithms than fixed exploration in both heuristic component design and complete solver generation. Particularly, it matches the optimal values on KP and reduces the gap on OBPP from 2.112\% to 1.199\% while using 42.5\% fewer search requests. Moreover, the learned exploration policies transfer effectively across different COPs with the assitance of the experience world.

*Jaeho Hwang,Yujin Oh,Juhyeon Shin,Juhye Lee,Sungroh Yoon,Uiwon Hwang*

TL;DR: OriTTA利用源模型的几何信息为每类设置轴向与理想点，单样本在线适配时对齐特征并抑制半径，保持少量历史样本并可自动恢复，展现出在CIFAR和ImageNetC上的最佳准确度且无坍塌风险。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 在在线、无标签测试流中，一次仅接收单个样本且类别顺序无序，传统置信度选择会导致错误预测；而数据污染只改变特征分布，判别器不变，导致单样本估计不稳定，迫切需要适应方法能在极低批量且无标签条件下稳定迁移。

Method: 构建源模型固定几何目标：先根据源模型生成每类合成原型的均值特征，并以其为起点选取新的“原点”，从此原点出发为每一类别赋予轴向与理想点；在适配过程中，只对预测类的输入特征进行轴向对齐与半径惩罚；维持每类仅一个历史样本以限制缺失类导致的漂移，并在重分类检测到灾难性坍塌时恢复模型。

Result: 在不需要针对每种设置调参的前提下，OriTTA在CIFAR-10-C、CIFAR-100-C、ImageNet-C上批量1时均实现了所有16种组合中最高的平均准确率；在ImageNet-C fog场景下，传统方法最多在100次实验中有两/三折崩溃，而OriTTA从未出现崩溃。

Conclusion: OriTTA通过原点重中化的几何约束，使单样本无标签适配在特征空间内具有良好的方向性与鲁棒性，显著提升了邮件测试流上的准确率，并防止了模型坍塌，成为目前最稳健的测试时适配方案。

Abstract: Test-time adaptation updates a deployed model on an unlabeled test stream. In practice this stream often arrives one sample at a time, with the classes in no particular order. A single sample then provides limited information for estimating distribution-level statistics, and selecting samples by confidence can admit predictions that are wrong yet confident. A corruption, however, shifts the features while leaving the decision regions of the frozen classifier unchanged. We therefore propose Origin-recentered Test-Time Adaptation (OriTTA), which constructs fixed geometric targets from the source model and adapts selected features toward them. Seen from the origin of the feature space, the classes lean toward a common direction. We choose a new origin to bring class scores closer together while keeping it near the mean feature of one synthetic prototype per class. From this origin, prototype directions spread more evenly, and each class obtains an axis and an ideal point before any test data arrives. During adaptation, OriTTA aligns selected features with their predicted class axis and penalizes excessive radius, keeps one input per predicted class in memory to limit feature changes for classes absent from the stream, and resets the model when re-classifying this memory reveals collapse. Without tuning for each setting, OriTTA achieves the highest mean accuracy at batch size 1 on CIFAR-10-C, CIFAR-100-C and ImageNet-C in all sixteen combinations of four backbones, two protocols and two stream orders. On ImageNet-C fog, recent methods collapse in up to two thirds of one hundred runs, while OriTTA never does.

</details>

Abstract: System identification, namely the inference of mathematical representations of dynamical systems from measured data, is fundamental to engineering diagnostics, prognostics, and decision-making. Bayesian filtering offers a principled framework for probabilistic state estimation but traditionally relies on prescribed transition and observation models. Recent advances in deep Bayesian filtering address this limitation by embedding learnable representations within probabilistic state-space formulations, enabling data-driven identification of unknown dynamics while retaining physical structure and uncertainty-aware inference.
  This tutorial provides a systematic introduction to deep Bayesian filtering for nonlinear structural system identification, focusing on the interplay between learned dynamics, physical inductive biases, and posterior inference. The Deep Markov Model serves as a data-driven reference, while the Physics-guided Deep Markov Model (PgDMM) and Neural Extended Kalman Filter (Neural EKF) illustrate complementary approaches based on amortized variational inference and differentiable Kalman filtering, respectively. Their capabilities are examined through dynamic state estimation and joint dynamic state and parameter estimation on a three-dimensional shear-frame benchmark with hysteretic Bouc-Wen connections and sparse acceleration measurements. The results highlight important distinctions between measurement-conditioned reconstruction and open-loop forecasting, demonstrate how physical structure can improve latent-state interpretability without necessarily ensuring predictive fidelity, and reveal differences in the robustness of response and parameter estimation. Practical guidelines for model construction, training, evaluation, and deployment are provided, together with an outlook on remaining challenges in uncertainty calibration, identifiability, and real-world validation.

</details>

We ask when these scores carry information that the clean softmax output lacks. A label flip requires the logit margin to be at most the square root of two times the logit displacement, and at a fixed margin, the displacement predicted errors at AUROC 0.515. The same inequality gives a label-free split-conformal no-flip certificate, never violated in 84 runs. The large negative CAG on OCTMNIST persists under full fine-tuning and in-domain training and is partly a class-prior effect: correcting the prior recovers about 40% of the gap.

Abstract: Two-sided matching systems, such as admissions pipelines, allocate opportunities jointly. Reducing a protected at tribute's influence usually requires encoder retraining. We study a closed-form linear projector that does this at inference time. For markets that stay vertically ordered before and after the edit, we bound the mean welfare change by the edit-induced appeal shift and an explicit remainder. A probabilistic refinement replaces worst-case constants with a data-dependent deviation term. A pre-deployment screen combines a verticality test with a welfare sweep and returns PASS, KILL, or OUT-OF-SCOPE. An exact tier requires every opportunity to rank applicants by appeal, and a graded tier admits near-vertical markets. On 30 held-out synthetic markets, the screen agrees with every pre-specified regime verdict, with no observed false PASS. On 60 exactly vertical markets, no bound is violated, and the probabilistic bound is informative in 69% to 87% of configurations. On 2700 configurations from three realistic markets, which do not reach the exact tier, no plug-in bound expression is violated. A closed-form factorization stays within graded scope in 90% to 100% of instances, whereas two-tower and graph neural network encoders fall outside it in every instance. Architecture choice thus appears to affect whether a post-hoc edit is admissible.

</details>

Abstract: Graph-structured representations of text data have become foundational across machine learning, yet it remains unclear how to systematically construct a graph from a given dataset for a target task. Choices about which entities become nodes, which relationships become edges, and what text each node contains can substantially affect downstream performance. In practice, finding optimal constructions often requires expert knowledge or manual trial and error. We introduce TACO-Graph, a decision tree framework for learning which graph construction choices are most effective for a given dataset and task. Specifically, we focus on text-attributed graphs (TAGs) constructed from multi-attribute tabular data. TACO-Graph enumerates valid TAG representations, scores each using fast proxy evaluation on small representative subsets, and trains an interpretable decision tree on those scores. Across five datasets spanning citation networks, product reviews, and bibliographic relationships, our decision trees predict construction quality on separately drawn evaluation samples with mean Spearman $ρ$ above $0.9$ for node classification and above $0.8$ for GraphRAG retrieval. Our trees systematically identify which constructions perform better across both GNN and GraphRAG tasks, and the full pipeline is released as an open-source library to support evaluation on new tasks and datasets at https://github.com/AmiriHayes/text_attributed_graphs. TACO-Graph provides a systematic and interpretable guide for determining how to convert tabular data to text-attributed graphs for downstream tasks.

Abstract: Optimizing expected rewards and reachability probabilities in parametric Markov chains (pMCs) is central to the analysis and synthesis of probabilistic systems with loops. While traditional symbolic synthesis approaches--such as parameter lifting and rational function generation--suffer from scalability bottlenecks, gradient-based optimization emerges as an effective alternative. However, existing gradient-based techniques still rely on symbolic gradient computation--such as solving large linear systems--over the global state space, inheriting scalability limits. Finite-horizon approximation is possible but introduces uncontrollable bias.
  In this paper, we introduce a scalable Monte Carlo gradient estimator based on a measure-theoretic generalization of the log-derivative trick. We prove that our estimator provides unbiased gradient estimates under almost-sure termination and standard regularity conditions. Crucially, our method avoids global state-space materialization and linear system solving entirely: it operates purely along sampled trajectories, requiring only black-box evaluations of local transition probabilities (or densities) and their derivatives. Unlike existing pMC techniques that are fundamentally restricted to finite discrete systems, our measure-theoretic framework naturally extends to continuous and general $σ$-finite state spaces. Empirical evaluations on synthetic discrete pMCs and continuous-state case studies demonstrate that our method scales to state spaces far beyond the reach of existing solvers, significantly reducing execution time without introducing bias.

These limitations raise a key question: how can heterogeneous neighborhood information be effectively leveraged for category discovery without any novel-class supervision? To answer this question, in this study we propose HeteGCD, a new statistics-aware dual-prototype learning perspective for generalized category discovery on heterophilous graphs. The key idea is to examine both statistics of local attribute differences and neighborhood class distribution to extract discriminative information in feature and probability spaces simultaneously. Specifically, a gated dual-stream encoder integrates both node attributes and local attribute-difference statistics to learn informative node representations. Afterwards, a dual-prototype classifier combines feature-based similarity with distribution-based consistency to support category discovery. Experiments on five heterophilous graphs show that HeteGCD achieves competitive performance against 24 baseline combinations.

Abstract: Multivariate time series forecasting plays a critical role in Web and cyber-physical systems, where large-scale services and IoT infrastructures generate complex temporal data. Transformer-based models have achieved strong empirical performance in this domain, yet the principles governing the expressive capacity of their learned representations remain insufficiently understood. Moreover, many existing models rely on simplified decoding strategies, including channel-independent modeling and direct projection heads, which limit their ability to capture complex multivariate dynamics. To address these challenges, we propose RankFormer, a novel encoder-decoder architecture for multivariate time series forecasting. We establish a rank-based analysis framework to investigate how tokenization, embedding, and attention designs affect the representation capacity of time series Transformers. Our analysis reveals that patchification increases the potential representation capacity by aggregating multiple temporal observations into each token, while subsequent encoding operations determine how much of this capacity is realized. Guided by these insights, we develop a rank-enhancing encoder with Hourglass Attention to improve rank stability during information interaction. Furthermore, we introduce the Interactive Progressive Decoder (IPD), which improves future trajectory modeling through global-guided cross-channel interaction and progressive refinement. Extensive experiments on real-world long-term forecasting benchmarks demonstrate that RankFormer achieves competitive performance against state-of-the-art methods. Abstract: Contextual dueling bandits learn from pairwise comparisons, the same feedback used to fine-tune language models from human preferences. Standard algorithms explore aggressively and can show users two poor options. We study conservative contextual dueling bandits, which require that, at every round, the cumulative score of the better displayed item stay above a $(1-α)$ fraction of a trusted baseline's. An item's score is its probability of beating the best available item, so the constraint depends only on utility differences and needs no absolute rewards. This also changes how safety can be certified: we show that transferring the safety test of the conservative bandit algorithm CLUCB unchanged can incur linear regret, even when the baseline's utilities are known exactly. For linear utilities, we develop MaxInP-BC, which certifies scores through utility gaps and, when it must fall back, pairs the baseline with an informative partner so that fallback rounds still drive learning. It is safe with high probability at all times, with regret $\tilde{O}(d\sqrt{T}/κ)$ plus an additive cost of conservatism that does not grow with the horizon. For general utility classes, we develop BTIGW-BC, which turns the guarantee of an online logistic-regression oracle into cumulative safety certificates. It is safe at all times, makes finitely many fallbacks, and has sublinear regret whenever the oracle's logistic regret is sublinear. In experiments, both satisfy the constraint, which their unconstrained counterparts often violate, at a moderate cost in regret.

Abstract: Backpropagation (BP) remains the predominant algorithm for training deep neural networks, but it is biologically implausible and suffers from the backward locking problem, constraining parallelization across layers. Direct Feedback Alignment~(DFA), which propagates the global output error directly to each hidden layer via fixed feedback matrices, has emerged as a promising alternative for addressing these limitations. However, a significant performance gap persists between DFA and BP, which we attribute to the limited ability of fixed feedback matrices to capture the nonlinear transformation from hidden representations to the network output. To address this limitation, we propose Nonlinear Direct Feedback Alignment (NDFA), which constructs each hidden-layer feedback using a lightweight nonlinear surrogate subnetwork. To enhance the effectiveness of the feedback throughout training, we further propose a learning strategy to update the surrogate subnetwork, thereby better approximating the underlying nonlinear transformation. Extensive experiments across residual networks and vision transformers on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, SVHN, and ImageNet datasets demonstrate that NDFA consistently outperforms existing DFA-based methods and substantially narrows the performance gap to BP, while maintaining efficient parallel training.
