Abstract: As executable environments for language agents scale, generating and verifying their rollouts becomes increasingly expensive. Under finite rollout budgets, this raises an important question: can data management at the environment level make agentic reinforcement learning (RL) more efficient by organizing, evaluating, and scheduling task instances together with the environments that determine their tools, states, transitions, and verification? To address this question, we introduce EnvSched (Environment-aware Scheduling), an online scheduler that learns from rollout history. EnvSched assigns scheduling value through two complementary modeling choices: exploitation prioritizes tasks likely to provide informative reward differences, while coverage spreads each batch across underobserved environments and interaction patterns. EnvSched organizes environment--task pairs as nodes in a dual-relation graph, with within-environment edges capturing task semantics and shared tool, state, and verifier structure, and cross-environment edges capturing abstract execution and reward topology. By propagating historical rollout statistics along both edge types, it predicts within-task reward variance. These predictions guide a scheduler that allocates each batch to tasks with high predicted reward variance and underobserved environments and tasks. Environment quotas and a similarity penalty further limit concentration and redundancy, yielding informative and diverse batches without additional pilot rollouts. In RL on Qwen3-8B, EnvSched achieves an average score of 48.81 across BFCL-v3, tau-Bench, and ACEBench-Agent after 40 training steps, exceeding the highest score attained by random task selection after 120 steps. It also improves downstream performance across model sizes at the same training budget.

Abstract: Weight decay and explicit weight-norm constraints improve optimizer performance in language model pretraining, yet a unified understanding of why such control helps and how much is needed remains incomplete. We analyze norm control in Muon over the entire training trajectory using the \emph{accumulated effective learning rate}, $C_T=\sum_{t<T}η_t/\|W_t\|_F$, where $η_t$ is the nominal learning rate and $η_t/\|W_t\|_F$ is the effective learning rate relative to the weight norm. For bounded update directions, accumulation over any training interval bounds relative weight displacement. We show that vanilla Muon can accumulate only logarithmically, potentially limiting exploration and helping explain its slower empirical progress, whereas MuonW and MuonH guarantee at least linear accumulation under the stated conditions. Going beyond bounded-norm control, we show that sufficiently slow norm growth guarantees faster accumulation than the logarithmic worst case, opening up less restrictive optimizer designs.
  Based on this insight, we propose MuonGS, which uses Gram--Schmidt (GS) projection to remove the radial component of each Muon update without fixing the weight norm. With bounded update directions and a constant nominal learning rate, we prove that the weight norm grows at most proportionally to $\sqrt{t}$ after $t$ steps. Consequently, MuonGS guarantees $C_T=Ω(\sqrt{T})$, with accumulation sustained even in later training. Further, for smooth scale-invariant objectives we establish its $O(T^{-1/2})$ stationarity guarantee. Abstract: We explore how to build performant large language model (LLM) tool-use agents that complete complex, multi-step tasks. Specifically, we study how data composition in synthetically generated tool-use environments affects the training of such agents. To do so, we propose the SynthAPI pipeline, which explicitly controls the diversity of generated environments along the axes of tools, tasks, and the databases on which the tools operate. By systematically varying SynthAPI's generation controls in 458,752 tasks across 849 trained models of four different sizes, we derive a scaling law that predicts downstream tool-use agent generalization based on the scale and composition of the synthesized environments. These predictable relationships reveal several lessons for scaling tool-use agent data. As data budgets grow, scaling tool and database diversity becomes more effective than increasing the number of tasks, and increasing data complexity yields larger gains. Scaling according to these findings yields agents that outperform models trained on data from prior work in large-scale tool-use agent and synthetic environment generation across four evaluation benchmarks.

Abstract: Relations compose through the entity they share - Alice is Bob's mother and Bob is Carol's father, so Alice is Carol's grandmother - and the order matters, since a father's mother is not a mother's father; verifying a composed relation means closing the loop. We show that if each entity's coordinate frame may be rotated independently (per-entity frame freedom), three things follow. In the pure-gauge sector, relations that share no cycle cannot interact at all. The only gauge-invariant score of a triangle is a class function of its holonomy $H = T_{ij} T_{jk} T_{ki}$ - a Wilson loop. And the only equivariant update of an edge is $U = Ψ(H)\, T_{ij}$ with $Ψ$ conjugation-equivariant. Edge attention by Wilson loops is therefore not one design among many but the unique form the symmetry permits. Three communities hold pieces of this form: the commuting-operator embeddings of knowledge graphs are its abelian (commuting) sector; AlphaFold's triangle update and the Edge Transformer have its shape - an edge updated through the third vertex - without its constraints; and lattice QCD builds it equivariantly on lattices. We instantiate the equivariant sub-family on general relational graphs as holonomy attention. With no training and composition alone, it infers unobserved $S_4$ relations on sparse graphs at 0.88 with a fifth of the edges corrupted, at or slightly below specialist synchronization algorithms, where every commutative architecture is capped at 0.083; its holonomy score flags corrupted triangles at AUC 0.99 where commuting scores are capped at 0.76 by a second selection rule, and it separates clusters without labels.

Abstract: Test-time adaptation helps a deployed model respond to changing inputs, but the same intervention can rescue one batch and damage the next. Updating normalization statistics, for example, can improve corrupted images yet fail when a batch contains mostly one class. Average accuracy hides these failures and cannot tell a model when to use an intervention. Target labels can guide this decision, but they are scarce and some choices are already clear. We introduce PORTAL, a controller that chooses when to deploy an intervention, which inputs to label, and when to stop querying. Each available prediction rule is a candidate, and its predictions are fixed before current feedback arrives. Every acquired label therefore evaluates all candidates on the same input. PORTAL combines these shared comparisons and directs queries toward candidates that can still overtake the current leader. It stops when the remaining labels cannot change the choice or when the possible accuracy gain no longer justifies another query. Our analysis links the required feedback to accuracy gaps and shared mistakes, explaining why some choices need much less evidence. Experiments show that this control turns an intervention that loses accuracy overall into a useful choice on selected batches. It prevents severe failures caused by changes in arrival order and preserves gains across complete corruption streams. When a fixed rule already works well, PORTAL uses little feedback to retain its performance. Periodic label checks reveal when past evidence needs refreshing and help the controller recover as conditions change. The acquired labels also train stronger candidates for future batches, and selection brings nearly all of their accuracy gains into deployment. The same feedback therefore improves both the choice made now and the predictions available to later batches.

</details>

Abstract: The feasibility of on-board, real-time proximity assessment to railway hunting initiation under uncertainty arising from vehicle/track and other operational conditions is assessed. In this context, a novel method that classifies hunting proximity into one of several prespecified speed proximity intervals is introduced, based on a grey nonlinear vehicle model with parameters estimated from on-board acceleration measurements and model-based estimation of the Maximum Lyapunov Exponent (MLE) as a hunting proximity measure. The method's performance and robustness are assessed via Monte Carlo simulations using a MATLAB-based multi-body vehicle model and four operating scenarios. The method is shown to achieve excellent performance achieving 97.08 % classification accuracy, which marks a significant improvement over a potential alternative. Its robustness to faulty suspensions and hollow-worn wheels is also demonstrated, although certain performance compromise is observed under worn-track conditions.

Abstract: We develop a multichannel sampling and reconstruction framework for multidimensional principal shift-invariant spaces based on a finite-dimensional polyphase representation. Periodic nonuniform shifts and derivative measurements are modeled as analysis channels, leading to a frequency-domain linear system relating the measurements to the polyphase components of the underlying coefficient sequence. Stable recovery is characterized through uniform singular-value bounds on the associated polyphase matrix, and explicit synthesis kernels are obtained from its Moore Penrose left inverse. The formulation applies to both separable and genuinely non-separable generators and includes several classical one dimensional sampling formulas as special cases. For irregular derivative measurements, where the periodic polyphase structure is no longer directly available, we develop an iterative reconstruction method based on local bivariate Hermite interpolation followed by orthogonal projection onto the shift-invariant space. A sufficient contraction condition is derived to guarantee geometric convergence of the iteration. Numerical experiments demonstrate the reconstruction performance for separable and non-separable generators and illustrate robustness to additive measurement noise and sampling-location perturbations.

</details>

Abstract: Functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS) offers a promising approach for monitoring the hemodynamic correlates of neural activity in naturalistic settings. However, existing modeling methods for fNIRS are largely confined to small-scale, single-session data with low sampling rates, driven by high heterogeneity in acquisition devices and optode montages. In addition, the complex spatiotemporal structure of fNIRS signals further restricts model scalability and transferability across settings. As a result, it remains unclear whether a single fNIRS encoder can learn representations that are usable across subjects, tasks, devices, and montages. Here, we show that a montage-aware masked autoencoder, pretrained on 12 public fNIRS datasets spanning 268 subjects and 567 recording hours, learns generalizable neurovascular representations that transfer across three external datasets. Our fNIRS foundation model, MtReNIRS, jointly encodes the slow hemodynamic nature of fNIRS, chromophore identity and cortical location by mapping heterogeneous montages onto a shared anatomical reference. MtReNIRS achieves cross-subject performance on par or better than fully supervised baselines and excels in few-shot within-subject calibration, where it outperforms prior state-of-the-art fNIRS models, while remaining robust to sparse montages. Our results indicate that montage-aware self-supervision yields physiologically grounded, transferable fNIRS representations, providing a scalable foundation for label-efficient brain-computer interfaces.

Abstract: The achievable gains of multiple-input multiple-output (MIMO) systems depend on channel state information (CSI), whose acquisition consumes pilot and feedback resources for every user in every coherence interval. Meanwhile, the propagation environment that shapes the channel persists. Exploiting such information will help to greatly reduce the overhead of CSI acquisition. This paper investigates the spatial MIMO channel rendering problem, which learns a latent beamspace channel scene from sparse observations and renders the long-term transmit and receive beam pair power map at unobserved user locations. Motivated by 3D Gaussian splatting in visual scene rendering, the proposed MIMO Gaussian splatting (MIMO-GS) represents the scene with pairs of receive and transmit 3D Gaussians, projects them from the user and base station onto the corresponding beam domains, and accumulates the beam associations through beam pair splatting. On a simulated urban scenario and measured indoor channels, MIMO-GS renders 5.5 times faster than ray tracing without a calibrated replica. Applied to multiuser beam management, the rendered maps allow users and transmit beams to be scheduled without sounding while accounting for the inter-user interference, and the net sum rate reaches 98% of the genie-aided benchmark, 19% above a position-based predictor.

Abstract: Channel state information (CSI) prediction forecasts future channel states from historical observations, supporting transceiver design and resource allocation with reduced measurement overhead. However, existing deep learning predictors mainly encode channel knowledge in model parameters, which can limit their ability to represent diverse patterns of channel dynamics across scenes. In this paper, we propose RAG-CSI, a retrieval-enhanced Transformer framework that augments prediction with relevant historical channel knowledge. Specifically, we construct a global--local CSI knowledge store containing representative observations across scenes and historical observations from the current serving scene. We further develop joint state--dynamics retrieval (JSDR), which jointly characterizes channel states, phase dynamics, and amplitude dynamics to retrieve relevant CSI sequences. A Transformer predictor then uses cross-attention to fuse the retrieved knowledge with the current CSI history and predict future CSI with limited additional model parameters. Experiments on a multi-scene CSI dataset show overall improvements exceeding 10\% across all five evaluation metrics over the best baseline for each metric, including a 15.42\% reduction in normalized mean squared error.

</details>

Abstract: Continuous monitoring of body-surface temperature distributions can provide useful information for wearable health and thermal-condition monitoring. However, practical daily-use wearable systems for distributed temperature sensing remain difficult to implement because multiple sensor nodes require power delivery and data collection across a garment. Conventional wiring increases system complexity, reduces placement flexibility, and can compromise the wearability of garment-integrated sensor arrays. To address this issue, we present a conductive-textile-based wearable temperature sensing system in which the textile itself serves as a shared bus for both DC power delivery and data communication. Each sensor node is implemented as a compact, battery-free circular board with a diameter of 21 mm and a weight of 0.9 g, integrating a commercial temperature sensor and custom circuitry for UART/DC superposition. The shared textile bus enables flexible node placement without individual wiring while supplying power to each node. Address-based half-duplex UART with master polling enables collision-free multi-node communication. To demonstrate the system-level capability, we implemented an 18-node sensor array on a garment worn by a mannequin and developed a real-time temperature mapping viewer. In a thermal scanning demonstration using warm air from a heat gun with a set temperature of approximately 50 °C, the 18-node array captured spatiotemporal temperature variations over the garment surface at a refresh rate of 99.2 Hz. These results show that the proposed conductive-textile bus can support real-time, high-refresh-rate distributed temperature mapping, providing a practical basis for garment-integrated body-surface monitoring and future multi-sensor wearable systems.

Abstract: A frequency-agile radar with a large digital array moves the spatial frequency of its look direction across the beams of a spatial FFT every time it changes carrier, so a beamspace adaptive processor must decide where to put its window at each hop. Because the beamspace transform is orthonormal, the output of the processor depends only on the span of the window, and a hop changes nothing but where the window sits. Centring the window exactly on the fractional bin of the look direction keeps the look vector fully inside it at every carrier, for the price of one phase ramp before the FFT. On a 32 by 32 array over 4 to 8 GHz the look bin drifts by 1.6 to 6.9 bins for look directions from u=0.2 to 60 degrees. Rounding to the nearest bin costs up to 1.34, 0.74 and 0.50 dB of look-direction capture for 3 by 3, 5 by 5 and 7 by 7 windows. In a three-jammer scenario with 41 carriers, a window frozen at the lowest carrier loses 39 to 60 dB at the worst carrier for a look direction of u=0.55, the exact window beats nearest-bin placement by 0.31 to 0.65 dB there, and the advantage reverses for a nine-beam window near broadside, where nearest-bin was 0.61 dB better.

</details>

Abstract: A beamspace minimum variance distortionless response (MVDR) processor adapts a window of B beams instead of all N elements of a large array, trading a subspace loss that falls with B against a covariance estimation loss that rises with B for a fixed number K of training snapshots. We give the subspace loss of a DFT beam window in point jamming in closed form: exactly for any jammer power, and in the high interference-to-noise ratio (INR) limit through the Dirichlet kernel of the array. Combined with the known scaled-Beta law of sample matrix inversion (SMI) with a fixed transform, it gives a window size B*(K) for unloaded SMI. On a simulated 32x32 array with carriers from 4 to 8 GHz, noise jammers and Billingsley land clutter, the exact form is within 0.04 dB of the clairvoyant subspace loss for 25- and 49-beam windows at jammer levels from 10 to 50 dB. For a 9-beam window whose three columns are taken by three jammers near the target's elevation, the clutter that the form leaves out costs up to 5 dB. With the nominal K, B*(K) picks the lowest-loss window in all 30 cases with more than one runnable window. The rule is an oracle: it needs the jammer parameters or the true covariance, and errors of 0.25 bin rms in the jammer directions add a mean loss of 0.04 dB. With diagonal loading the best window was larger still wherever the rule chose more than the smallest window.

Abstract: PPG is the main technology for wearable heart rate (HR) monitoring. However, motion artifacts during intense physical activity reduce its reliability due to spectral overlap with the cardiac signal. This study investigates whether severely contaminated PPG signals can yield reliable HR estimates without auxiliary sensors by leveraging prior knowledge of HR dynamics. We acquired simultaneous PPG and ECG signals from 21 subjects during a maximal cycloergometer test. Using Leave-One-Subject-Out cross-validation, we built HR evolution priors from the ground-truth ECG and applied Dynamic Time Warping (DTW) to align normalized HR curves and estimate a barycenter average. DTW aligns normalized HR curves with minimal error across all subjects, validating robust population-based priors. For each test subject, we combined the PPG Power Spectral Density (PSD) with the prior distribution to generate a "posterior PSD". We identified and ranked candidate HR trajectories using heuristic search within the "posterior PSD" and selected the most likely path. Our approach reduced mean absolute error (MAE) in BPM relative to the ground-truth ECG by a median of 63.3% (IQR: 47.3%-74.6%) compared to a PPG+accelerometer benchmark. These results show that physiological priors and DTW-based alignment enable robust, PPG-exclusive HR monitoring during high-intensity exercise.

Abstract: Accurate acquisition of full channel state information (CSI) is essential for multiple-input multiple-output orthogonal frequency-division multiplexing (MIMO-OFDM) wireless systems, while dense pilot-based estimation incurs substantial overhead. Directly minimizing the prediction error over raw CSI coefficients is fragile in realistic propagation environments, since small-scale fading, mobility, local scattering, and small spatial perturbations can induce large phase-sensitive variations. Nevertheless, wireless channels contain strong spatial, temporal, and frequency-domain correlations that can be more predictably represented in a latent propagation-field domain. Motivated by this observation, we propose a joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA)-inspired field world model, defined as a latent predictive model of the wireless propagation field, for sparse-pilot channel reconstruction. The model maps CSI observations into latent time-frequency tokens, predicts masked or future latent field states from historical multi-band context, and fuses the predicted latent prior with sparse noisy current pilots to reconstruct the full MIMO-OFDM channel. Ray-tracing experiments show that the proposed method improves normalized mean square error (NMSE) over pilot-only and direct reconstruction baselines, supporting latent field prediction as an effective prior for sparse-pilot channel acquisition.

This study investigates the use of permanent scatterers (PSs) as opportunistic references for estimating residual radiometric gains without dedicated calibration targets. The PS-based calibration (PSCal) framework combines amplitude-based scatterer selection with iterative maximum-likelihood gain estimation. Results were observed to be consistent with measurements from the BIOMASS Calibration Transponder (BCT) installed in New Norcia (Australia). These results demonstrate the potential of carefully selected P-band PSs to complement dedicated calibration infrastructure for relative radiometric monitoring and the detection of processing anomalies.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a prominent data-driven approach to optimal control, but often requires substantial amounts of data and provides limited guarantees when encountering out-of-distribution state-action pairs. In this paper, we propose an alternative approach grounded in the well-established Hamilton-Jacobi framework in optimal control theory. Rather than requiring an explicit analytical model of the system dynamics to evaluate the Hamiltonian, we replace this with a data-driven Hamiltonian (DDH), which we generalize to a broad class of optimal control problems. In contrast to standard RL approaches, the DDH provides a principled mechanism, requiring only minimal prior system information such as a Lipschitz constant, for quantifying and accounting for uncertainty in regions not covered by the data, enabling the derivation of controllers with performance guarantees. Additional prior system knowledge, when available, can be readily incorporated to improve performance. For high-dimensional systems, we further introduce a physics-informed neural approximation of the value function, trained using a novel procedure that generates worst-case fictitious rollouts evaluated through the DDH. On an LQR problem, the DDH reaches near-optimal cost on every dataset we test, including random data on which offline RL methods remain far from optimal, and needs far fewer samples than online RL. On a nonlinear lunar lander benchmark, the DDH achieves an optimality gap to a trajectory-optimization reference more than $3\times$ smaller than CQL and FQL, and matches the performance of SAC with approximately $37\times$ fewer samples. These results establish the DDH as a promising framework for data-driven optimal control, particularly in settings where available data are limited in quantity or quality.

</details>

Abstract: Periodic power fluctuations from artificial intelligence (AI) training workloads can excite oscillations in power systems. This paper proposes a model predictive control (MPC) framework that exploits short start-time delays to mitigate power fluctuations at the computing source. By coordinating the relative phases of independent training jobs, the proposed method reduces the superposition of oscillatory power components without interrupting running jobs or requiring additional power-smoothing hardware. To obtain a tractable optimization formulation, cosine and sine projections are employed to quantify the dominant oscillatory power components, and their magnitudes are used to construct a convex quadratic power-smoothing objective. The receding-horizon framework updates launch decisions as new workload information becomes available while maintaining scheduling feasibility under limited computation time. Numerical results demonstrate reductions of 74.39\% in mean-square power fluctuations within the 0.2--2-Hz band and 46.6\% in power standard deviation, with an average launch delay of 2.188~s. All launch deadlines are satisfied, and the maximum end-to-end computation time is 0.390~s, below the 0.50-s MPC update period.

Power adjustments disturb the electrolyte temperature and superheat, which may lead to electrolyte freeze-out or yield loss. To address this limitation, this paper proposes a Thermal-Mass Coupled (TMC) dynamic model. In the illustrative PBDR and IBDR case studies, the nonlinear reference-model assessment shows that the TMC model achieves net economic improvements 122.4% and 325.1% higher, respectively, than the ETP model. A PID cascade with the same gauge saves about as much only when tuned by a standard rule at its recommended setting from a constant-load step test, difficult in an operating plant; the NMPC needs no such test and keeps its performance with its initial model and default weights after plant expansion and network split. Abstract: Partially observable restless multi-armed bandit models have been studied for dynamic resource allocation problems under uncertainty. This paper analyzes three-state restless multi-armed bandits with partial observability. These are called collapsing bandit problems if the state of an arm (bandit) is perfectly observable when it is scheduled for allocation. The state is not observable when the arm is not scheduled. The planner has a budget constraint on the scheduling decisions in each time slot. The planner's goal is to maximize expected cumulative long term discounted reward. We study the Whittle index policy. To analyze index policy, first the $N$-armed collapsing bandit problem is decoupled into $N$ single arm problems, and convexity and monotonicity of the optimal value function in belief vector are derived using monotone likelihood ratio (MLR) ordering on state transition dynamics and monotone reward structure assumption.
  We next show that the optimal policy is of threshold type in belief under sufficiency conditions on the model. These sufficiency conditions depend on Lipschitz bound on the value function in the belief. We further prove the indexability for an arm using Lipschitz bound on subsidy sensitivity with minimal restrictive assumption on the transition probability model and discount factor $β.$ Our results generalize to three state and strengthen existing results on threshold policy and indexability with weaker model assumptions. We provide index computation algorithm and we present comparison of the Whittle-index, myopic policy and round-robin policies in the simulation study.

Standard safety notions are based on the idea that an unsafe region of the state-space must be avoided at each instant. In contrast, we study a sequential safety notion which is based on avoiding a tuple of target regions of the state-space. Abstract: When coordinating connected and automated vehicles at an unsignalized intersection, the crossing order and the vehicle trajectories must be decided jointly. For a fixed crossing order, the problem can be formulated conveniently in a bi-level form where each vehicle solves its own optimal control problem, whose optimal value is a function of its entry and exit times, and a coordinator optimizes these times subject to precedence constraints. We compute the first and second order sensitivities of lower-level problems, which are required to solve the upper-level problem, by means of algorithmic differentiation using the real-time software package \texttt{acados}. This implementation exploits tailored algorithms to obtain a computationally efficient implementation of the optimal coordination algorithm. We validate our implementation in numerical simulations that demonstrate both real-time feasibility and effectiveness of our algorithm.

In controlled privacy--utility studies, the noise needed to protect shared states erases their detection value before it buys meaningful privacy, whereas counts stay useful even under strong privacy protection. These findings support a practical rule for banks: use what they already record, and formally protect the little that must move.

Abstract: Owners of data in cloud object storage cannot directly check whether the provider still keeps every object unchanged. Existing research mostly checks blocks inside files or relies on third-party auditors, and less attention has been given to checking each object against a public blockchain reference while measuring the cost. A full-data audit downloads every object and compares it with a saved hash list, which is slow and only as reliable as that list. We propose a public auditing system in which the SHA-256 hash of each object and its name forms a leaf of a Merkle tree, so the whole dataset is represented by one Merkle root. This root is stored in an Ethereum smart contract as a trusted public record, while the objects stay with the provider. An auditor downloads an object, rebuilds the root using a short Merkle proof and compares it with the stored root, marking the object as intact, modified or missing. In five experiments on an S3-compatible store and a local Hardhat blockchain (normal audit, single- and multiple-object tampering, deletion and scalability), the system detected 15 of 15 single modifications, 2,205 of 2,205 modified objects and 830 of 830 deleted objects with no false alarms. The proof needed only 14 hashes for 10,000 objects, so checking one object took about 10 ms and a 100-object sample about 150 ms regardless of dataset size, while a full audit of 10,000 objects (14.3 s) took about as long as downloading them (13.5 s). Storing the root cost 91,863 gas, 252 times less than storing 1,000 hashes on-chain. The system thus shows exactly which objects were modified or are missing, with its main savings coming from checking selected objects.

</details>

Across 18 model-server pairs with valid tool execution, Log-To-Leak achieves an attack success rate (ASR) of 62.64-100% and conditional logging accuracy (LA) of 76.55-95.64%; 16 pairs have absolute target-task completion changes of at most 6.63 percentage points. Three evaluated defenses leave residual ASR above 55% on GitHub MCP with GPT-4o, while an instruction detector flags 85.53% of benign descriptions. These results show that successful task completion can coexist with unauthorized post-hoc disclosure and motivate host-controlled authorization for secondary tool calls.

Abstract: Cybersecurity education has predominantly focused on software and network security, under the assumption that the underlying hardware is trustworthy. Threats introduced through the global semiconductor supply chain, together with vulnerabilities arising during design and integration, challenge the assumption that hardware can serve as an unquestioned root of trust. Sensor-enabled systems and machine-learning accelerators further expand the hardware attack surface. The undergraduate curriculum of electrical and computer engineering and computer science programs has not evolved accordingly. Core courses cover the design and the functionality of hardware while the security aspects remain overlooked, and dedicated hardware security (HS) courses are elective, scarce, and often restricted to graduate students. At the University of Florida, fewer than 7% of the undergraduate electrical and computer engineering students register for the introductory HS course each year. We present an integrated and economical approach that disseminates fundamental HS concepts within existing core courses through contextual learning. Nine hands-on modules were developed such that each security concept exercises an associated design concept of the core course, as a component of that course and not as a course of their own. Doing the security work is how the student exercises the design topic, so the modules are built to strengthen the host course and not only to add to it. Three were assessed across three offerings of the embedded systems course through one course survey and two conducted by an external evaluator, with 25 respondents. We report what those instruments measured and the limitations of the approach.

Abstract: Multi-turn jailbreaks turn dialogue safety into a trace property: a violation may become identifiable only when user intent and assistant behavior are considered across multiple turns. Existing guard models largely classify individual messages or exchanges and return risk scores or labels, without executable safety semantics or a formally checkable decision trail. We present SpecGuard, a training-free, specification-driven framework for monitoring multi-turn LLM interactions. SpecGuard uses a fixed atomic-proposition contract to map natural-language observations into causally scoped, evidence-backed facts, and expresses forbidden interaction patterns as Linear Temporal Logic specifications. Each specification is compiled into a symbolic finite automaton that incrementally consumes the resulting dialogue trace. The same monitoring mechanism supports buffered runtime checking and retrospective auditing, while accepting runs produce replayable witnesses that identify the triggered specification, supporting facts, and relevant dialogue turns. Experiments on multi-turn safety benchmarks show that SpecGuard consistently outperforms representative open-source guard models while maintaining controlled false-positive rates. These results demonstrate a practical path from heuristic risk classification toward formally specified, verifiable, and auditable LLM safety monitoring, without learned detector parameters or access to the protected model.

</details>

Abstract: Summing vectors is a basic task in differentially private data analysis. Standard private sum algorithms clip vectors to some bound, sum them, and add noise scaled to the bound. The differential privacy literature has developed a deep library of noise addition mechanisms, but it offers few tools for privately choosing a clipping bound based on the data. We introduce a method that privately chooses a clipping bound by optimizing residual coherence, the directional alignment of vectors exceeding the bound, against the variance cost of additional noise. We prove a close relationship between residual coherence and bias for a general class of heavy-tailed data regimes and show empirically that, across several datasets, our method outperforms existing baselines.

</details>

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) rely on proprietary system prompts to enforce task directives, safety policies, and access controls. These prompts are vulnerable to query-based leakage attacks, where black-box adversaries extract confidential instructions through adaptive, multi-turn interactions. Existing defenses often assess queries independently, missing malicious behavior that emerges across interactions.
  We present AdvGuard, a model-agnostic framework for detecting and mitigating system prompt leakage. AdvGuard models cross-query relationships using a bounded semantic interaction graph, statistically filters candidate components, and converts them into line graphs for graph convolutional network (GCN) classification. This approach captures multi-hop patterns in query similarity that distinguish coordinated extraction from benign workflows. A lightweight prompt-level detector provides protection before sufficient interaction history accumulates, and the framework supports black-box LLM deployments.
  In mixed-query evaluations, AdvGuard improves recall by up to 36% over the strongest baseline while maintaining a false positive rate of at most 3.4%. In multi-turn workloads, it achieves 97.23%-99.1% F1 with 100% recall. Leave-one-attack-family-out tests yield 99.98% mean recall and 99.99% mean F1, with no observed false positives. Integrated into the adaptive PLeak and LeakAgent attack loops, AdvGuard limits exact-match leakage to 0.00-0.01 and substring-match leakage to 0.01-0.09 across evaluated victim models. These results demonstrate robust detection of unseen attack families and substantially reduced prompt reconstruction.

Abstract: AI-assisted vulnerability discovery can produce technically plausible findings faster than maintainers can validate, coordinate, and safely disclose them. We present FedTrust-CVDv2, a federated coordinated vulnerability disclosure framework that separates evidence validation, interorganizational coordination, human governance, and permissioned auditing. Abstract: Attack logs and red-team reports show targets the attacks used against them, to help them repair their defenses. Prompt injections against large language models (LLMs) are plain text, so such records also hand targets a working copy. Drawing on social learning theory and work on the victim-offender overlap, we study what targets do with that copy in Tensor Trust, an online game whose players defended LLM-guarded accounts, attacked one another's, and could read every attack they received. Using all 563,349 attack records, we compare each received attack with matched attacks that circulated among similar players at the same moment but never reached the target. Reuse mainly followed success: within a week, targets attacked other players with 10.0% of the attacks that had broken their defense, versus 0.03% of matched attacks (adjusted difference: 8.8-10.0 percentage points). Near-misses, whose replies began with "access granted" but that scored as failures, were reused about as rarely as failures: reuse tracked whether an attack paid off, not how its reply read, consistent with payoff-biased social learning. Breaches were also followed by defense changes, after which same-family attacks became 24.5 percentage points less successful against the target, relative to other defenders. Reuse came mainly from players already attacking, not newcomers. Previously received texts accounted for 23.2% of the successful hijacks we analyze. The attack history thus served both repair and reuse; we derive testable hypotheses for how AI security tools should disclose attacks, such as describing the technique without the full text.

</details>

We present FIDES-Live, an operational extension of FIDES from generated strategy concordance to the audit of sequential agent traces. These observations motivate reporting concordance, evidence coverage, activity, and financial outcomes separately. They do not establish long-run trading skill, semantic faithfulness of explanations, or causal benefits from audit feedback.

Abstract: Global narrative streams exhibit noisy, fragmented, and asynchronous amplification bursts, making direct waveform comparison unreliable for recurrence analysis. This paper introduces Peak-DNA, a framework for identifying recurrent propagation morphology in global narrative streams. The proposed framework transforms hourly GDELT activity into normalized macro-peak episodes through denoising, macro-peak consolidation, and temporal alignment, and compares propagation morphology using DTW-based similarity analysis. Using GDELT narrative streams, we analyze five high-amplification narrative domains: Iran, Defense, AI, Space/Elon, and Quantum/PQC. The results show that amplification events repeatedly converge toward morphology-level propagation patterns within each narrative domain. DTW-based similarity analysis further demonstrates strong intra-domain structural compactness despite heterogeneous publication dynamics and severe observational noise. These findings suggest a measurable morphology layer beyond semantic content or anomaly intensity, providing an initial structural foundation for recurrence-aware narrative monitoring, propagation family discovery, and longitudinal Narrative Genome analysis.

Abstract: Third-party community-based bots are central to how social platforms such as Discord work. They moderate content, manage roles, play music, and support AI-assisted conversations across millions of servers. Yet when a bot is installed in a server, it can access messages, user profiles, and server metadata concerning members who did not participate in the installation decision, with no standardized way to communicate the associated security and privacy implications. In this paper, we analyze 23,102 third-party Discord bots and 183,142 user reviews spanning 49 detected languages and five years of feedback (2021-2026). We employ LLM-based knowledge distillation and fine-tune a DeBERTa-based classifier to identify candidate security and privacy (S&P)-relevant reviews. After manually validating the complete classifier-positive set, we retain 8,832 reviews identified as S&P-relevant, representing an observed pipeline yield of 4.82%, and map them to a 31-theme S&P taxonomy extending prior work. Our results show that S&P-related reviews focus heavily on Data Sharing (30.5%) and Data Collection (16.2%). Confession and anonymity bots have pipeline-identified SPRRs above 50%, more than ten times the ecosystem-wide pipeline yield of 4.82%. Star ratings may obscure the frequency and valence of S&P-related discussion, while conventional metadata features, such as permission count and administrator status, show only weak associations with user-reported S&P concerns. We then build BotLens, a proof-of-concept auditing framework that combines review-derived S&P signals with bot metadata to provide on-demand decision support for the community-based bot ecosystem. BotLens exposes six tools through a Model Context Protocol server, supporting bot-level checkups, server-wide comparison, and integration with AI agents.

Abstract: Many decentralized exchanges use automated market makers (AMMs) to trade assets against liquidity pools. Modern AMMs allow third-party contracts, called Hooks, to customize this process. However, Hooks can impose additional deductions that fee breakdowns based on standard pool parameters do not itemize. We present, to the best of our knowledge, the first large-scale empirical study of the production Hook ecosystem, focusing on these undisclosed settlement charges. Our study covers 7,873 Hook deployments, 50,011 pools, and 296M pool events on Uniswap v4 and PancakeSwap Infinity across Ethereum and BNB Smart Chain (BSC) through August 1, 2026. We develop a bytecode analysis that reconstructs how Hooks change traders' payments and receipts, separating charges from exchanged assets and standard fees. We then reconstruct historical settlements to measure charging frequency and economic burden. We find that direct settlement adjustment is the dominant Hook capability, supported by 71.37% of registered Hooks. Charging capability is widespread and often exercised: 24.30% of Hooks can impose undisclosed deductions, and these deductions occur in 59.23% of accounted swaps involving such Hooks. These charges generate millions of dollars in revenue, with $3.97M recorded across Hooks with available token prices. These findings reveal a gap between standard fee displays and actual trading costs, motivating explicit disclosure of Hook deductions alongside standard fees.

</details>

The margin has a closed form, takes about $85\,μ$s per certificate, yields a minimum-evidence attack, and identifies task-relevant directions the detector cannot see. On a 17-channel industrial telemetry benchmark, a detector that flags 82.8\% of random single-channel corruptions is first-order blind on 64.7\% of task/subset pairs in an audit of one- to four-channel subsets. Task-conditioned manipulations cause more than twice as many falsely reassuring errors as generic stealthy ones, and more of them evade the alarm on four of five splits, mainly at small budgets. Used to prioritize audits on held-out subsets, the margin finds a median of four verified failures in ten checks, compared with none for random order and two for alarm-based ranking, and performs comparably to the task-gradient baseline. It also ranks vulnerable configurations of the learned GDN and TranAD detectors and of a five-source crop-yield panel.

Rather than relying on isolated single-pass judgments, PrivacyEvalClaw treats evaluators and analysis modules as tools, adaptively gathers evidence, investigates uncertain disclosures, and reconciles conflicting signals before producing a final judgment. Our primary experiments use pairwise LLM-as-Judge evaluation of complete trajectories, showing that PrivacyEvalClaw achieves favorable win rates against competing baselines across outcome, process, and overall quality, while producing explicit reasoning traces that make the evaluation process more transparent and auditable.

Chen's withdrawn 2024 quantum algorithm for LWE and a proposed repair of its post-processing would, if correct, reach only a many-sample, small-noise LWE regime that the failure-exponent hypothesis already covers for exponents below $1/2$, and no deployed parameter set. Abstract: Stateless post-quantum authenticated key exchanges such as post-quantum WireGuard hedge against unexpected cryptanalytic advances by combining a lattice KEM with code-based Classic McEliece. However, recent attacks have led Germany's BSI to advise against McEliece, which leaves these protocols with lattices alone. HQC, the code-based KEM that NIST selected, could restore diversity, but its keys do not fit in a single packet, so a server would have to buffer multi-packet handshakes. This would leave an attack surface for memory exhaustion, which is what WireGuard's stateless handshake was designed to avoid in the first place. McTiny demonstrated that stateless key encapsulation for Classic McEliece is possible by streaming key blocks one-at-a-time. However, HQC's encapsulation does not split as easily into individual packets, as cyclic polynomial multiplication, unlike matrix-vector multiplication, is inherently non-local.
  We present HqcLethe, a stateless key exchange that streams an HQC encapsulation in packet-sized pieces. It uses the Chinese remainder theorem to split the polynomial multiplication into independent remainders, one per packet, and masks them so that they reveal no more than an ordinary HQC ciphertext, which we prove in Rocq with SSProve; we also verify session secrecy, server authentication and forward secrecy in ProVerif. Client puzzles bound CPU exhaustion, and HqcLethe removes memory exhaustion: on a Raspberry Pi 3B+, each worker runs in a fixed 1.2-1.6 MiB. At category 5 it sends 27x fewer bytes and uses 9.0x less server CPU than McTiny, the earlier stateless code-based design.

</details>

Abstract: Payment channel hubs (PCHs) enable private cross-chain payments, but existing protocols remain difficult to extend across heterogeneous blockchains. Their payment flows often depend on how particular blockchains represent transactions, authorize channel updates, and settle balances. Some protocols do not explain how their channels are implemented on actual blockchains. Supporting a new ledger or execution model requires a corresponding channel realization and may also require protocol adaptation and security analysis. We present UniPCH, a framework with two layers that separates native channel execution from private cross-chain payment logic. The lower UniPC layer defines common state transitions and security requirements. It separates task authorization from confirmation of the complete channel state. We provide realizations for UTXO ledgers and account ledgers that support smart contracts. At the upper layer, we develop selective re-signing blind conditional signatures (S-RBCS) to coordinate conditional authorization across hubs. Using S-RBCS, we construct a payment protocol that coordinates different channels in four phases. Our analysis establishes local UniPC security, griefing resistance, economic atomicity, and session unlinkability. We implement a Go prototype and evaluate the running time and bandwidth of each phase under different network conditions and system parameters. Results show modest computational overhead from the common interfaces. Most evaluated configurations run faster than comparable schemes under network delay. A full refresh ablation shows that reusing valid task evidence reduces computation and communication costs in the account realization.

Abstract: Log messages are employed to identify anomalies in IoT systems and have been widely used across many applications. To enable collaboration among infrastructures belonging to different organizations, federated log anomaly detection has been thoroughly investigated for this purpose, often assuming the availability of ground truth log templates. In particular, we evaluate the performance of five distinct parsing algorithms in combination with aggregation techniques that allow a unified agreement of event-template mappings across all participating clients, a prerequisite for federated log anomaly detection. In total, 6,720 distinct distributed configurations were tested for this purpose.

Abstract: Audit-based certification bounds the violation rate of an LLM agent by auditing a random fraction $\varepsilon$ of actions and reweighting the audited labels; its validity is meant to rest only on the defender's audit coin. We show that it also rests on a confidentiality property: the coin must not interfere with the agent's view before the action commits, at a time the agent chooses. We call this pre-commit noninterference (PNI) and measure its violation by $D_{\mathrm{A}}$, the total-variation distance between the agent's views under the two coin values. PNI is necessary and sufficient for an unbiased certificate against adaptive agents, the worst-case bias is $(1-\varepsilon)D_{\mathrm{A}}$, and no procedure that sees only audited labels can pay less. Leakage composes across steps, a resource shared across episodes opens a channel of computable size, and raising the audit rate cannot restore validity. On a monitor we built, a silent audit changes no output but adds latency that tool timestamps expose, and the agent can enlarge it. A real LLM agent follows an injection that names a visible audit notice (generic, tool, and timing clauses have no effect); over that channel the plain certificate fails on measured labels and, by a post-hoc analysis, in a live run on a pre-registered schedule, while a silent audit restores it. A fresh coin per audited call closes the timing channel; padding only narrows it.

Conclusion: 文章提出了一种实体中心威胁模型，揭示了现有汽车网络安全体系在应对恶意设计车辆和被重新利用组件时存在的真实性与功能一致性盲点；通过行为-定位矩阵和交互模型为恶意行为提供了细致的分类与分析路径。

Abstract: Automotive cybersecurity commonly assumes benign end-product manufacturers and design authorities, focusing on supply-chain threats, external attacks, and compromised network participants observable through misbehaviour. This assumption overlooks malicious-by-design vehicles that remain authentic and protocol-conformant while performing undeclared sensing, communication, or context-triggered actuation. We formulate an Entity-Centric Threat Model spanning three subcomponents. The first, Malign Entity Archetypes, describes four types of malign entity: peer-benign, purpose-malicious vehicles; compromised vehicles; reappropriated genuine components; and imitated vehicles or components. The model exposes two assurance gaps: authenticity does not establish declared-purpose conformance, while malicious functionality can evade detection based on behavioural history or peer-visible evidence. Reappropriated components can remain genuine and retain usable identities or capabilities outside authorised contexts, reducing the effectiveness of signal fingerprinting and certificate-based authentication. To structure this threat space, we introduce a Behaviour-Locus Matrix that maps covert, overt, and concealment actions across hardware, software, and network loci. For peer-benign, purpose-malicious vehicles, we further present a Behavioural Interaction Model that represents how hidden capabilities are contextually activated, realised through actions, manifested within the vehicle and its environment, and potentially observed or concealed.

</details>

Abstract: All traditional encryption schemes share a fundamental operational vulnerability: encrypted data must be decrypted before it can be used. Fully homomorphic encryption eliminates this vulnerability by enabling computation directly on encrypted data, but existing FHE schemes impose substantial practical constraints that limit deployment. This paper presents a formal security analysis of the Gribov-Kahrobaei-Shpilrain (GKS) private-key FHE scheme. GKS requires no bootstrapping or de-linearization, produces bit-exact decryption, supports native encrypted search, and scales to unbounded depth.

Abstract: Modern secret sharing protocols promise robust secret sharing in fully asynchronous Byzantine systems, but their practical costs remain difficult to compare across papers, assumptions, and workloads. The difficulty is not merely experimental: protocols with similar asymptotic guarantees often place very different work on the critical path through their choices of dissemination, verification, batching, and recovery. This paper turns that fragmented research landscape into a corpus-backed designspace analysis and systems evaluation of asynchronous verifiable and complete secret sharing. We derive a design sieve, select seven representative protocols, and implement them in a common Rust framework. In our experiments on the Grid'5000 platform, we evaluate honest-dealer and faulty-dealer stress executions using latency, throughput, message count, bandwidth, and aggregate CPU-time. The results show that message complexity alone poorly predicts latency, that optimistic paths can be dominated by recovery cost, and that batching or packing helps only when verification stays off the critical path. These findings turn protocol-by-protocol comparisons into design-to-cost guidance for choosing, implementing, and extending asynchronous verifiable and complete sharing protocols.

</details>

Abstract: Proprietary language models and their reasoning traces are vulnerable to API-based distillation, where an adversary trains a student model on the teacher model's outputs. To obtain evidence of distillation, model providers can watermark the teacher's outputs, aiming to leave a radioactive signal in the student's outputs. Existing black-box detectors search for such radioactivity using token-level statistics of the suspect model's outputs. However, token-level signals are not always detectable, for instance when the distillation traces are sampled at a low temperature. In this work, we discover the watermark basin, a persistent signal that enables more reliable watermark detection: during fine-tuning, watermarked teacher traces embed a distinct, key-specific geometric signal in parameter space that lies orthogonal to the ordinary task-specific fine-tuning subspace. Building on this, we introduce Watermark Basin Alignment (WM-Basin), a black-box watermark detector that queries the suspect model through its API, without access to its weights or logits. WM-Basin maps generated text to gradient representations using a fixed clean probe model, projects out the ordinary fine-tuning task directions, and tests whether the residual gradient aligns with the watermark basin induced by the watermark key. We evaluate WM-Basin extensively across diverse teacher models and student architectures (from 135M to 8.2B parameters) distilled on mathematical reasoning. At a strict 0.1% false-positive rate, WM-Basin detects 79% of distilled models at n-gram=1 and 45% at n-gram=3, substantially improving over the state-of-the-art token-level baseline (29% and 1.3%, respectively). Because the watermark signal resides in a persistent basin, it survives post-distillation modifications including quantization, pruning, and reinforcement-learning post-training.

</details>

The results show that: when the envelope is authentic, strong labels consistently reduce the semantic breach rate, whereas weak labels are not necessarily better than an unlabeled baseline; when the envelope can be forged, the benefit of strong labels is weakened and its direction varies across frontends; and memory has a larger effect than any label manipulation: success memory raises the semantic breach rate while failure memory lowers it, and most combinations that contain both experiences return to the no-memory baseline. Accordingly, this paper refines the envelope layer from a single admission channel into two levels, an admission channel plus a context assembly surface, and argues that its security boundary is not a fixed property of labels but a dynamic outcome jointly shaped by label claims, frontend permissions, and historical memory.

Abstract: Data memory-dependent prefetchers (DMPs) are a microarchitectural optimization recently introduced in mainstream desktop and server CPUs. Although DMPs offer performance improvements, they have been shown to enable side-channel leakage of secrets, even from constant-time code that has been hardened against Spectre attacks. Unfortunately, existing defenses either incur significant performance overhead or, as we show, fail to prevent all DMP leakage.
  In this work, we address this gap by designing a defense that is both secure and efficient. First, we propose a classification of DMP leakage, categorize existing and novel attacks, and analyze which leakage classes are mitigated by existing defenses. We validate this analysis empirically by implementing the first open-source DMPs on a RISC-V processor, showing that the only existing defense that aims to preserve DMP performance fails to protect against entire leakage classes. Next, we propose SecDMP, a provably secure and efficient hardware-based defense. SecDMP requires secrets to be marked in memory and ensures that DMPs never encode these secrets in the microarchitecture---without compromising performance for public data. We formally prove that SecDMP introduces no leakage beyond a baseline semantics without DMPs. Notably, when combined with speculative-execution defenses, SecDMP restores complete microarchitectural security for constant-time code. Finally, our empirical evaluation shows that SecDMP prevents all DMP leakage while incurring minimal performance overhead and hardware cost.

</details>

Abstract: AI chatbot platforms have become one of the most popular web interfaces users interact with online, routinely receiving personal and intimate information shared under an expectation of privacy. Yet, on the web application side, these platforms have the ability to embed third-party analytics, advertising, and session replay scripts that operate silently alongside the chatbot, creating an underexamined channel for conversational data leakage towards third parties. We conduct the first systematic large-scale measurement study of contextual leakage in AI chatbots, auditing the web interfaces of 42 general-purpose and domain-specific (health, legal, and education) platforms. Beyond standard network interception, we instrument the browser at the JavaScript engine level to attribute each data flow to the exact user action that triggered it, and the DOM capture mechanism used to retrieve its content, a granularity invisible to network-layer analysis alone. We find that 21 of these platforms leak conversation titles to third-party trackers, while nine leak verbatim user prompts. Beyond content, we observe a substantial amount of chatbot platforms sending user emails to third-party trackers, enabling the association of conversational context with a user's identity. Our findings cast traditional web privacy issues under a new light, highlighting the privacy issues that are inherent in how users interact with modern AI chatbots.

Abstract: HTTP method semantics are a common boundary between safe retrieval and state-changing operations; however, deployed web applications do not always enforce this boundary consistently. In this paper, we study HTTP method interchangeability, a behavior where a POST request can be rewritten as a GET request to the same endpoint, with POST body parameters moved into the query string, while preserving an equivalent observed response. We present Method Interchange Detection Engine (MIDE), an automated pipeline that crawls web applications in a browser, records POST requests generated by page loads and user-interface interactions, converts supported POST bodies into GET candidates, and compares replayed responses with the original POST response. To avoid counting generic or parameter-independent responses as interchangeable, MIDE also sends a no-query GET baseline. We treat as high-confidence evidence of interchangeability only the cases where the converted GET matches the POST, while the baseline does not. We perform a large-scale unauthenticated experiment on the Tranco top 10k. We find high-confidence interchangeable requests on 407 of 10,000 tested sites (4.1%), 407 of 3,200 sites with captured POSTs (12.7%), and 407 of 2,734 sites with replayed POSTs (14.9%). A targeted Cross-Site Request Forgery (CSRF) token-replay check finds cases where invalid tokens are rejected for POST but accepted for the equivalent GET. We also observe Web Cache Deception (WCD) conditions and cacheable GET responses for interchangeable request instances. These results demonstrate that method interchangeability is observable in real web applications and can expose method-scoped defense gaps.

</details>

Abstract: Model quantization has become a fundamental technique for enabling the efficient deployment of large-scale deep neural networks (DNNs) in data-intensive applications by reducing model size. Despite its widespread adoption, the impact of quantization on training data privacy --- particularly vulnerability to membership inference attacks (MIAs) --- remains largely unexplored. In this paper, we present the first comprehensive empirical study of privacy risks in quantized DNNs, evaluating eight state-of-the-art MIA attacks across six representative quantization methods. Our results reveal that practical quantization levels (4-8 bits) have limited impact on membership privacy risks, with quantized models remaining largely as vulnerable as their full-precision counterparts. Although aggressive low-bit quantization (e.g., 2-bit) can provide meaningful privacy gains, these benefits come at the cost of substantial utility degradation. To understand the underlying causes of these observations, we systematically analyze how different quantization strategies affect model behaviors and outputs, and how these changes influence privacy vulnerability. Beyond evaluating attack risks, we further investigate whether existing privacy defenses developed for full-precision DNNs can effectively protect QNNs. Our results show that these defenses provide only limited additional privacy protection when applied to quantized models, while further amplifying utility degradation. Our study provides practical guidance for privacy-aware deployment of quantized DNNs and motivates the development of new attack and defense mechanisms for QNNs. Our code and datasets are publicly available at https://github.com/JeffffffFu/Priv-QDNN.

</details>

Abstract: We study deterministic causal quantization with pathwise error at most $\varepsilon$, using $ω$ shared representatives updated from transmitted addresses alone. For a midpoint realization with first-in, first-out replacement, we compare address entropy rates with process $\varepsilon$-entropy, the least entropy rate of any block quantizer meeting the same constraint. From $\varepsilon$-separated initialization, the gap is at most two bits per sample for every stationary source with finite scalar $\varepsilon$-entropy, without density, independence or mixing assumptions. A causal address-tree argument extends this comparison to any synchronized quantizer with cell overlap $m$, giving $\log_2 m$ bits. Lattice initialization with consistent boundary ownership realizes uniform grids with sharp one-bit scalar and process comparisons. Under an entropy condition and finite Fisher information rate $J$, the gap is at most $2J\varepsilon^2/(π^2\ln 2)$; general initializations add a term of order $ω\varepsilon\sqrt{J}$. The scalar grid estimate gives a nonasymptotic form of Rényi's theorem. Since a grid is causal, the price of causality is at most $\min\{1,2J\varepsilon^2/(π^2\ln 2)\}$ bits per sample, versus the high-resolution squared-error loss $\tfrac12\log_2(πe/6)$. These bounds certify $\varepsilon$-entropy brackets for autoregressive Laplace and Gaussian sources. For memoryless sources, the optimal causal rate equals the scalar $\varepsilon$-entropy.
  For a stationary process on finitely many $2\varepsilon$-separated centers, adding independent uniform noise of width $2\varepsilon$ gives exact Shannon lower-bound equality at every block length, attained by storing those centers. Abstract: Accurate CSI forecasting is essential for mitigating channel aging and supporting reliable downlink transmission in high-mobility wireless systems. In FDD systems, forecasting future DL CSI from historical UL observations requires joint temporal extrapolation and cross-band inference. However, defining historical UL as the input and future DL as the target does not specify how elapsed time and UL-to-DL band separation should be represented in a pretrained predictor's positional space. FAR-CSI establishes an FDD-aware representation of channel forecasting within a joint temporal and operating-band positional field. Within this field, historical UL observations and future DL targets occupy segregated regions according to their temporal and operating-band roles, preserving the distinction between observed UL context and unobserved DL prediction targets. This representation is instantiated through a unified structured routing rule that governs encoder positional assignment, decoder-field reconstruction, and future-DL target selection, ensuring the same FDD geometry is preserved from contextual encoding to prediction readout. Under matched backbone, initialization, and training conditions, FDD-aware routing reduces NMSE by 21.44\% relative to generic routing. Across the evaluated FDD baselines, FAR-CSI achieves higher CSI prediction accuracy and MRT-based spectral efficiency, while retaining its predictive advantage across mobility conditions, prediction horizons, reduced training-data regimes, and noisy historical observations.

Abstract: For any desired rate $R \in (0,1)$, and gap to list-decoding capacity $\varepsilon > 0$, we give an explicit $n^{O_{\varepsilon}(1)}$ time construction of Reed-Solomon codes over extension fields of size polynomial in the block length $n$ that can be list-decoded from a fraction $1-R-\varepsilon$ of errors with the optimal list-size of $O(\frac{1-R}{\varepsilon})$. We also give a simpler, strongly explicit construction of evaluation points for which the associated Reed-Solomon code can be list-decoded from $1-R-\varepsilon$ errors with list size $(1/\varepsilon)^{O(1/\varepsilon)}$. We provide a deterministic list-decoding algorithm for both constructions running in time $O_ε(1)\mathsf{poly}(n)$.
  In fact, our Reed-Solomon codes are near-optimal subspace-design codes over a subfield. Via known results, this directly implies the combinatorial list-decoding guarantee, as well as near-optimal list-recovery bounds. We also present a new reduction showing that the subspace-design guarantee also implies line decoding over the extension field itself, which then yields proximity gaps and mutual correlated agreement up to proximity parameter approaching the optimal $1-R$ radius.
  Compared to the recent algorithm of Brakensiek, Chen, Putterman, Zhang, and Zheng (2026), which list decodes every Reed-Solomon code over prime fields up to capacity, our approach achieves constant (in fact, near-optimal) list size rather than a polynomially growing one, at the cost of carefully choosing the code's evaluation points. In a companion paper, we show that these respective restrictions are unavoidable.

Abstract: We study the thermodynamic efficiency of message-passing decoders using the mismatch cost framework. This framework quantifies the excess entropy generated when a system operates away from the thermodynamically optimal initial distribution. It provides a computable lower bound on irrecoverable energy loss. The decoder is modeled as a main decoding program with local message updates implemented as subroutines. Memory is treated as an external copying subsystem whose thermodynamic cost is excluded. Studying the $[7,4]$ Hamming code transmitted over a binary erasure channel, we show how the channel-induced initialization statistics and the step-wise evolution of the algorithmic state distribution during decoding determine the total mismatch cost. We further show that different thermodynamically optimal state distributions, representing different underlying physical implementations of the decoder, can produce different thermodynamic costs for identical decoding dynamics. Conditioning the input distribution on correctable and non-correctable erasure patterns further reveals distinct thermodynamic signatures. These results demonstrate that thermodynamic cost is jointly governed by decoding dynamics, input statistics, and hardware characteristics. This points to opportunities for thermodynamically-informed decoder design to realize energy-efficient physical implementations.

</details>

Abstract: This paper studies the fundamental limits of bistatic integrated sensing and communications (ISAC) systems over vector channels. We first consider a class of parallel channels in which, on each component channel, the channel from the transmitter (Tx) to the communication receiver (Rx) is degraded with respect to the channel from the Tx to the sensing Rx, or vice versa, where the direction of degradation may vary across component channels. For this class of channels, we establish matching achievability and converse results, which yield a single-letter characterization of the capacity-distortion function $C(D)$. For parallel Gaussian channels with additive Gaussian states, the evaluation of the capacity-distortion-power function $C(D,P)$ under the squared error distortion measure reduces to a joint power and distortion allocation problem across the component channels. We then extend the analysis to general vector channels and derive single-letter lower and upper bounds on $C(D)$. In particular, for vector Gaussian channels with additive Gaussian states, we prove the optimality of Gaussian superposition coding without requiring a global degradation order between the communication and sensing channels, by combining the channel enhancement technique with a matrix extremal inequality based on Fisher information. This leads to an exact characterization of $C(D,P)$.

Abstract: Spatial degrees of freedom (DoF) are fundamental to the performance of multiple-input multiple-output (MIMO) systems. However, the use of conventional fixed-location antennas introduces disparities in the users' distances to a common base station, which makes the multi-user channel matrix more susceptible to rank deficiency and, consequently, a loss of spatial DoF. This paper shows that generalized pinching antennas can be used to design a new form of MIMO robust to the loss of spatial DoF. In particular, by deploying pinching antennas in close proximity to the users, the resulting multi-user channel Gram matrix can have a well-conditioned, almost diagonal structure, which reduces its sensitivity to the users' spatial deployment as well as the risk of rank deficiency. Two performance metrics, namely the rank-deficiency probability and the outage probability, are used to evaluate the performance of pinching-antenna-assisted MIMO transmission. The presented analytical and simulation results demonstrate the capability of pinching antennas to preserve spatial DoF and provide ubiquitous connectivity by effectively supporting users at spatially separated locations.

</details>

Abstract: Boolean functions are widely used in cryptography and coding theory. A key challenge lies in finding the weight spectrum for all $n$-variable Boolean functions of degree at most $r$, also known as the Reed--Muller code $\RM(r,n)$. In 2023, Carlet and Solé determined the weight spectra of $\RM(n-c,n)$ for $c=3,4$, and conjectured the weight spectrum of $\RM(n-c, n$) for $n\ge2c$. The weight spectra of $\RM(n-5, n)$ and $\RM(n-6,n)$ were determined by Carlet in 2024 and by Lou and Wang in 2025, respectively, confirming the Carlet-Solé conjecture for the cases $c=5,6$. Very recently, Leuenberger et al. and Wei et al. investigated the weight spectrum of $\RM(7,14)$ independently, and there are eight potential weights which have not been determined. Up until now, the weight spectra of the Reed--Muller codes $RM(r,n)$ are still unknown for $r=3,...,n-7$. In this paper, we determine the exact weight spectrum of $\RM(m-7, m)$ for every $m\ge 14$. By combining affine geometry and Fourier analysis, we exclude the conjectured eight weights for $\RM(7,14)$. We then extend the exclusions to all higher dimensions using a geometric transfer. Consequently, we provide a counterexample to the Carlet-Solé conjecture.

</details>

We study 1-generator $(1,2)$-quasi-cyclic (QC) additive codes and their exact quantum distances. For composite lengths $n=\ell m$, an ancestor-lifting theorem transports dual words of the folded ancestor to the length-$n$ code. A two-layer decomposition for $\ell=2$ and a CRT splitting for odd $\ell$ yield dimension laws and explain equality, escape, and mixed-branch behavior in the certified examples. A palindromic criterion ensures symplectic self-orthogonality, while reversal pairing and a symplectic Gram rank formula describe broader construction families. Exact distances are established by information-set decoding screening, Brouwer--Zimmermann computation on the additive dual, and independent witness verification. We certify twenty-one record-breaking binary quantum codes across twenty entries of Grassl's code table, with twenty-eight certified members in total. Propagation improves thirty-four further entries, and explicit constructions are supplied for three table-tying GV entries marked Code Missing Construction. Six additional candidates are reported only with rigorous upper bounds and heuristic screening evidence. The results connect divisor-lattice structure in additive quasi-cyclic codes with reproducible certification of exact quantum distances.

Abstract: Root mean square (RMS) delay spread is a key parameter characterizing temporal dispersion in radio propagation channels. Its estimation from measured power delay profiles (PDPs), however, depends strongly on the adopted power threshold. This paper investigates the sensitivity of RMS delay spread estimates to relative PDP thresholding in a time-varying millimeter-wave channel at 33 GHz. The analysis uses wideband channel sounding measurements conducted in a dynamic road environment with moving vehicles. The temporal evolution and empirical cumulative distributions of RMS delay spread are evaluated for thresholds ranging from -20 to -50 dB. Reducing the threshold increases the median RMS delay spread from approximately 140 to 168 ns and the 90th percentile from 200 to 210 ns. Both statistics converge at lower thresholds, with -40 dB providing a practical convergence level. The results emphasize the importance of threshold selection for reliable channel characterization and consistent comparison of measurements.

</details>

Abstract: Fluid antenna systems (FAS) leverage continuous reconfigurable apertures to transcend the spatial limits of fixed-position arrays, yet acquiring full-aperture channel state information (CSI) from sparse observations remains a formidable bottleneck. In this paper, we establish a continuous-field formulation for FAS by recasting channel estimation as a stochastic inverse field-reconstruction problem constrained by the projected Helmholtz model. Through electromagnetic information theory (EIT)-based spectral leakage analysis, we demonstrate that finite-aperture reconstruction induces an intrinsic convolutional relationship in the spectral domain, rendering the estimation task a fundamental frequency-domain inverse deconvolution problem. To solve this, we propose a U-shaped Fourier neural operator (UFNO) specifically tailored for noisy, underdetermined wireless environments. Furthermore, we leverage the Landau--Pollak theorem to derive an aperture-dependent EDoF bound that guides the spatial mode budget, and employ a physics-aware Sobolev loss to promote consistency in both channel values and spatial variations. Unlike conventional learning-based approaches, a single frozen UFNO can be directly evaluated at unseen spatial--frequency resolutions without resolution-specific retraining or explicit output interpolation. Extensive simulations show that our framework significantly outperforms state-of-the-art benchmarks in accuracy while achieving orders-of-magnitude lower inference latency than iterative solvers at high spatial--frequency resolutions.
