Abstract: We study variable-length feedback (VLF) coding over $K$ additive white Gaussian noise (AWGN) channels whose noise variances are unknown to both terminals but lie in a known interval. The receiver selects one channel at each time, conveys the selection over noiseless feedback, and must learn the channel qualities while decoding, to avoid overusing suboptimal channels. This setting, which we call the VLF-Bandit problem, combines VLF coding, universal decoding, and bandit channel selection. We develop two joint coding and channel-selection schemes. The VLSF-Bandit scheme is a single-phase variable-length stop-feedback (VLSF) scheme based on a Gaussian random codebook, a score formed from a ratio of likelihood mixtures, and the asymptotically optimal $\text{kl-UCB}^{++}$ bandit algorithm applied to the received power. The VLF-Bandit scheme sends the message in packets by Schalkwijk--Kailath transmission, uses its normalized outputs as noise samples to learn the variances, and lets its exploration packets carry message bits. We prove second-order achievability bounds for both schemes, which are first-order optimal. In the logarithm of the achievable codebook size, the magnitude of the $\log N$ coefficient, with $N$ the average decoding time, is a baseline of one plus a channel-exploration term for VLSF-Bandit. For VLF-Bandit, this magnitude is smaller, with a universal-decoding term replacing the baseline and a smaller channel-exploration term. The VLF-Bandit bound improves on the universal VLF bound of Yavas and Tan (2025) for $K=1$ and on the bound of an explore-then-commit strategy whenever $K\geq2$.

</details>

Abstract: This work investigates how site-specific digital twins (DTs) can support wireless localization using simplified distributed infrastructure. We propose PRISM (Probabilistic Refinement using Inverse learning and a Site-specific Model), a DT-guided probabilistic localization scheme with single-antenna access points (APs). Localization is formulated as a probabilistic inverse problem in which a conditional proposal distribution over UE locations is learned from multi-node channel impulse responses (CIRs). The proposal generates candidate locations, with its spread regularized during training and DT consistency used to reweight candidates and refine the estimate at inference. The DT serves as an approximate forward-consistency model, avoiding assumptions of exact channel fidelity or differentiability with respect to user position. Numerical experiments demonstrate gains over data-driven and classical fingerprinting benchmarks, while achieving a median error of approximately $0.7$ m using 10 single-antenna APs. Without retraining, PRISM remains effective under degraded inference-time DT features. PRISM also yields uncertainty estimates from its proposal covariance, with empirical $χ^2$ calibration approaching ideal behavior as APs increase.

Abstract: A Gaussian random wave field is sampled on a lattice within a bounded aperture. After displacement, re-indexing leaves a residual in the Voronoi cell. We study whether the empirical lag correlation has positive real part throughout the cell. For each realization in a single-output reciprocal link with noiseless over-the-air return, this is equivalent to the negative returned gradient being a descent direction for all residuals and differentiable losses with nonzero error signals. Return noise is treated exactly. For isotropic fields, ball-shaped apertures, unshifted lattice points, and dilations approaching the critical covering radius, every minimizer is a deep hole except with exponentially small probability. A Gaussian vector on the deep holes determines the limiting failure probability, with explicit covariance and lattice-independent limiting normalization. Square, hexagonal, simple cubic, and body-centered cubic lattices have two, three, four, and twelve sign classes. At fixed target failure probabilities below the critical limiting value, this covariance determines the exact leading aperture constant. When the margin and target failure probability vanish jointly, that constant is common to lattice shapes within each dimension. A cubic correction yields vanishing relative tail error in a moderate-deviation regime. A non-asymptotic two-sided aperture law has explicit constants. With a Rayleigh-faded specular path and positive diffuse margin, a failure floor occurs at every positive path power exactly when the cosine of its phase change is negative somewhere in the cell. The floor vanishes for all directions exactly when the covering radius is at most a quarter wavelength.

</details>

Abstract: This paper investigates cross-layer design for differentially private federated learning over cell-free massive MIMO networks with over-the-air (OTA) aggregation. Distributed access points (APs) collaboratively serve user equipments (UEs) through user-centric clustering, where clipped local model updates are perturbed with Gaussian noise and simultaneously transmitted over a shared uplink for OTA averaging at a central processing unit. Exploiting channel hardening, we show that thermal noise inherent in wireless reception can act as a privacy-masking resource, reducing or even eliminating the need for artificial noise. Based on this observation, we formulate a joint optimization problem over AP-UE associations, UE transmit powers, and artificial noise variances to minimize aggregation variance subject to per-UE zero-concentrated differential privacy constraints derived from an ergodic privacy accounting framework. Analysis reveals a regime-dependent structure: stringent privacy requirements favor low transmit powers and sparse AP associations to exploit thermal noise for privacy, whereas relaxed requirements favor larger clusters to improve aggregation quality. The resulting mixed-integer nonconvex problem is solved via alternating optimization combining difference-of-convex power allocation and greedy coordinate-descent clustering. Numerical results show up to 89% lower aggregation variance than conventional baselines and enable 97% test accuracy on MNIST with consistent gains on CIFAR-10. Under strict privacy budgets, competing schemes remain near random-chance performance, while under relaxed budgets they continue to lag significantly behind the proposed design.

</details>

Abstract: Near-field communication (NFC) sensor tags are promising for low-cost, scalable environmental monitoring due to their battery-free, maintenance-free operation. Recent advances in NFC reader garments, integrated with conductive textile coils, allow users to interact seamlessly with tags scattered across their daily environment. However, dynamically identifying and reading out a fluctuating number of tags remains challenging. If multiple tags speak and collide, they must randomly retry until an empty slot is found. Standard NFC identifies tags using time-slotted ALOHA, which assigns tags to fixed time slots. If multiple tags speak and collide, they must retry with a tag filter to avoid collision until all tags are identified. This rigid setup is fragile in dynamic settings; as tags enter and leave the communication range, the heavy overhead of slot re-negotiation and inevitable collisions restricts data throughput. Here, we propose an asynchronous NFC based on packet interval modulation. By treating minimal ID-only packets as pulses and encoding data in their intervals, we enable communication through silence. This ensures the channel remains mostly unoccupied, drastically reducing collisions even in high-density environments. Our simulations show that for ten tags, our method achieves a 4-fold increase in throughput compared to the ALOHA approach. Furthermore, the system remains robust, maintaining over 160 bps/tag throughput even under extreme fluctuations in the number of tags over 20.

</details>

Abstract: As connected applications -- from intelligence-enabled assistants and the smart homes to autonomous vehicles and large-scale Internet-of-Things deployments -- become increasingly pervasive, their performance, safety, and reliability depend more than ever on the underlying network. Designing and validating such systems therefore requires development environments that holistically account for both application and network behavior. While network simulators and emulators provide high-fidelity models of network behavior, connecting full-fledged applications to them remains difficult due to platform constraints, simulator-specific interfaces, and narrow single-purpose integrations. To address this gap, we present NSB2 (Network Simulation Bridge 2), an open-source network co-simulation middleware that relays payloads between application processes through a network simulator. NSB2 is composed of a central daemon and thin, embedded application- and simulator-side libraries for flexible integration, disentangled communication channels for unencumbered inter-process communications, and in-memory key-value storage for efficient payload caching and retrieval. NSB2 is designed to be lightweight, accessible, and extensible: it exposes streamlined interfaces supported in Python and C++, modular internal abstractions that let users swap or extend network simulation/emulation backends, flexible and configurable deployment modes, and integration helpers for the widely-used ns-3 and OMNeT++ network simulators. Our evaluation across multiple co-simulation scenarios shows that NSB2 introduces negligible memory and latency overhead, enabling researchers and developers to achieve richer, more realistic modeling of connected systems with minimum overhead.

This paper proposes a smart, real-time, energy-aware monitoring system architecture for IoT using a brokerless Data Distribution Service (DDS) middleware. Environmental and electrical metrics are transmitted with high reliability, achieving an average latency of 1.76 milliseconds and a throughput of 57.1 Mbps. Overall, the proposed architecture offers a transparent and low-latency framework for critical facility management.

Abstract: Low-Earth-Orbit (LEO) satellite networks are expanding broadband access, but major Real-Time Communication (RTC) platforms, including Google Meet, Zoom, and Microsoft Teams, still rely on service-point selection mechanisms that appear to be largely shaped by terrestrial-network assumptions. As a result, they may not consistently account for LEO-specific dynamics such as satellite topology and Point-of-Presence (PoP) changes, especially during in-flight connectivity. To characterize RTC service-point selection over Starlink, we measure RTC performance using a Canada-wide testbed and more than 24 hours of in-flight experiments. Our results show clear platform differences. Google Meet tends to align more closely with the serving PoP, consistent with broader observed edge coverage. Zoom follows a regional service-point selection pattern, while Microsoft Teams often remains tied to fixed service points. Moreover, we show that in-flight PoP handovers can shift traffic egress points mid-session, and that RTC platforms respond differently to these changes. In some cases, this interaction leads to suboptimal service-point selections and RTT inflation. Overall, our findings suggest that LEO connectivity can expose mismatches between Starlink egress dynamics and RTC service-point selection, motivating further measurement and LEO-optimized designs for service placement and selection.

</details>

Abstract: Reliable low-latency connectivity for real-time applications is a key requirement for 5G and future 6G non-terrestrial networks. Low Earth Orbit satellites enable global coverage but impose long propagation delays, rapid channel variations, and delayed feedback, which challenge conventional link adaptation algorithms designed for terrestrial networks. To address this problem, the paper proposes a Multi-Level Outer-Loop Link Adaptation (ML-OLLA) algorithm that assigns a dedicated target block error rate and an independent adaptation process for each transmission attempt. With ML-OLLA, early transmission attempts use higher-rate modulation and coding schemes (MCSs), while later ones use more robust MCSs. The core contribution of this work is a low-complexity data-driven parameter selection method that determines ML-OLLA parameters. Numerical results obtained with ns-3 show that the proposed ML-OLLA algorithm increases the system capacity by up to four times and reduces the minimum supported latency budget. In particular, for the 99.9% reliability target, ML-OLLA reduces the minimum supported latency budget from 60 ms to 40 ms compared with the baseline OLLA.

Abstract: Radio-frequency (RF) digital twins (DTs) enable site-specific wireless propagation prediction by explicitly reconstructing the physical environment and modeling electromagnetic interactions. Although physics-based DTs reduce reliance on exhaustive measurements, systematic bias relative to real radio data arises from environmental uncertainty, material inaccuracies, and geometric abstractions. Calibration using field measurements is therefore necessary but costly under limited sampling budgets. This paper studies active RF DT calibration, in which measurement locations are selected sequentially and in an online manner to estimate a calibration offset under strict measurement budget constraints. A physics-based RF DT pipeline is developed, integrating semantic geospatial data from OpenStreetMap, 3D modeling in Blender, and deterministic ray tracing in Sionna to capture dominant building and foliage effects. This RF DT is calibrated using path-loss measurements collected with software-defined radios. Multiple online calibration strategies that select radio measurement locations are compared, including random sampling, rank-structured sampling with balanced coverage, quasi-Monte Carlo ranked selection, progressive structure-aware allocation, and fully adaptive uncertainty-driven sampling with estimator stabilization. Results demonstrate that adaptive and structure-aware schemes significantly improve both mean and worst-case calibration accuracy, particularly in the low-budget regime for radio measurements.

</details>

Abstract: Network slicing is a key enabler for the coexistence of ultra-reliable low-latency communications (URLLC) and enhanced mobile broadband (eMBB) in 5G networks. However, allocating limited radio resources to heterogeneous slices while satisfying stringent quality-of-service (QoS) requirements remains challenging, particularly in the ultra-reliable regime where conventional effective-capacity-based approaches may lead to excessive resource allocation. To address this issue, this paper proposes a martingale-based resource block (RB) allocation framework for coexisting URLLC and eMBB slices. By constructing exponential martingale processes using the moment generating functions (MGFs) of the arrival and service processes and applying Doob's maximal inequality, explicit non-asymptotic upper bounds are derived for the URLLC delay violation probability and the eMBB backlog violation probability. Based on the resulting theoretical stability conditions, a low-complexity bisection algorithm is developed to determine the optimal martingale parameters, followed by a deterministic search algorithm to obtain the minimum RB requirements for both slices. Numerical results show that the analytical bounds effectively upper-bound the Monte Carlo simulation results. At a target violation probability of $10^{-6}$ for each slice, the proposed framework requires 44 RBs in total, satisfying the system capacity constraint of 50 RBs. Compared with the conventional effective-capacity baseline requiring 64 RBs, the proposed framework reduces total RB consumption by approximately 31.2% relative to the conventional effective-capacity baseline.

</details>

Abstract: Vehicle-to-vehicle (V2V) communication can improve road safety and traffic efficiency, yet its deployment remains limited by the need for dedicated communication hardware and supporting infrastructure. Meanwhile, production vehicles are already widely equipped with millimeter-wave radars for sensing. To unlock their communication potential, this paper presents RadarCom, a software-defined system that enables direct radar-to-radar communication on unmodified commercial off-the-shelf (COTS) automotive mmWave radars while preserving their native sensing functionality. The key insight is that mutual inter-radar interference, traditionally viewed as harmful, can be repurposed as a communication carrier. Based on this insight, RadarCom introduces slope-mismatch-based discovery, adaptive chirp synchronization, differential coding on range-FFT, and sensing-aware interference suppression to enable simultaneous communication and sensing without hardware modification, waveform redesign, or infrastructure support. We implement RadarCom on TI AWR2243 automotive radars and evaluate it under static, dynamic, and challenging real-world conditions. Results show an average effective data rate of 5.55 kbps with symbol error rates below 1.5%, while maintaining sensing F1 scores of up to 80.5% during concurrent communication. These results demonstrate the feasibility of transforming commodity automotive radars into dual-purpose communication and sensing devices.

Abstract: Human errors remain the leading cause of network outages. To prevent them researchers propose general verifiers that analyze the network behavior. Yet, their (symbolic) models struggle to accurately reflect network devices, they fail to scale to large networks, and report misbehavior, not what configuration line caused it. We attribute all three challenges to specifications that describe the intended behavior but not how operators intend to achieve it---the network's design: which protocols to run and how to combine them.
  We present Axiom, a language for expressing network designs, and a checker that finds every violation and the responsible commands. Operators state their design decision as rules over configuration commands. Axiom then compiles them into a single Datalog program that checks the entire network directly on its configurations, not on the state they produce. Our prototype checks a 754-router network within seconds. Ultimately, a design enforced on every keystroke could give network operators the integrated development environment that programmers have long enjoyed.

</details>

*Maksymilian Wojnar*

TL;DR: 通过离线训练的代理模型和样本-评分策略，大幅降低 CO‑SR 绿谱配置的在线计算复杂度，保持接近最优吞吐。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: IEEE 802.11 bn 中的空间复用方案具有巨大的吞吐潜力，但组合配置空间庞大导致实时最优化困难。

Method: 随机抽样生成 CO‑SR 配置，使用预训练的代理模型评估分数，选择得分最高的配置，无需在线探索或多步交互。

Result: 在 2–16 个接入点的未见拓扑上，方案恢复了 52–77% 的完美代理吞吐；使用 8192 个候选的完美样本-评分达到 95% 的上限吞吐。

Conclusion: 样本-评分方法结合离线训练的代理模型，在不进行在线微调的情况下，能够在实时环境中实现 CO‑SR 配置的近似最优，恢复了 52‑77% 的完美代理实现，通过 8192 个候选可达 95% 的理论吞吐上限。

Abstract: Coordinated spatial reuse (Co-SR) in IEEE 802.11bn increases area throughput through simultaneous transmissions, yet the combinatorial configuration space makes real-time optimization challenging. We propose a sample-and-score approach: draw random Co-SR configurations, score each with a surrogate model, and select the best. The surrogate is trained offline and deployed without online fine-tuning or sequential exploration, yet performs close to multi-armed bandit and heuristic baselines that need multiple interaction steps or extensive measurements. On unseen topologies with 2-16 access points, it recovers 52-77% of a perfect surrogate's throughput. A perfect sample-and-score approach reaches 95% of the upper bound throughput with 8192 candidates.

</details>

Abstract: Wake-up radios (WURs) reduce energy consumption by continuously monitoring the channel for a wake-up signal (WUS) while the main receiver remains asleep. Neuromorphic computing offers a promising approach to ultra-low-power WUS detection through sparse, event-driven processing. However, realizing these benefits requires spike-like inputs, which conventional orthogonal frequency-division multiplexing (OFDM) waveforms do not naturally provide. To enable neuromorphic WUS detection in OFDM-based systems, we introduce a neuromorphic wake-up signal (nWUS) that can be generated by a standard OFDM transmitter. The proposed design superposes frequency-domain vectors with linear phase progressions across the OFDM subcarriers, producing a time-domain waveform with sparse amplitude peaks at predefined positions. These peaks encode the wake-up signature in a spike-like representation that facilitates neuromorphic processing at the receiver. Building on this structure, we then propose a noncoherent neuromorphic detector that encodes received peaks into spikes and performs sparse correlation only upon spike events. Simulations over a 3GPP urban macro channel show that the proposed approach outperforms on-off keying (OOK) baselines in detection probability while achieving up to 43.5x lower computational cost. During idle periods, event-driven processing further reduces correlation operations by 86% relative to nWUS reception.

</details>

Abstract: Flow-based traffic classifiers trained on Ethernet data may lose accuracy when applied to Wi-Fi traffic. This paper proposes a five-stage procedure for selecting and constructing features that enable Wi-Fi flow classification using models trained only on Ethernet data. The procedure removes the transport protocol identifier, examines rate dependence, compares feature distributions and the information each feature provides for the application classes, reduces redundancy, and constructs normalized packet-size and temporal features. Ethernet and Wi-Fi traffic are simulated in ns-3, with Wi-Fi using Best Effort access category. Labelled Wi-Fi data from six development scenarios are used for feature design, while a separate scenario is reserved for final testing. The evaluation compares feature sets, examines threshold sensitivity, and analyses classification errors under different traffic conditions.

Abstract: Mobile Internet-of-Things (IoT) endpoints such as connected vehicles, wearables, and drones continuously cross heterogeneous network domains, creating handover-driven attack surfaces that static, cloud-anchored detectors cannot follow. However, three obstacles block real-time detection on such roaming endpoints: detection state is lost at every inter-domain handover; transmitting raw telemetry to a fixed detector exhausts the uplink and the device battery; and full-depth language model inference cannot meet millisecond latency budgets on constrained hardware. To tackle these challenges, we propose MAPLE-Sec, a mobility-aware partitioning framework that splits a compact, security-tuned language model across a roaming device and its nearest edge node. First, a trajectory-conditioned partition scheduler recomputes the split point from predicted handover timing, residual battery, and uplink quality. Second, a token-budgeted early-exit mechanism emits a verdict at the shallowest layer that meets a confidence threshold. Finally, a learned activation-compression codec migrates the intermediate inference state across edge nodes during handover without retransmitting raw telemetry. We formalize the partition-and-migration decision as an on-device constrained optimization solved in sub-millisecond time. Extensive experiments across the CIC-IoT and Edge-IIoTset corpora under both IID and non-IID roaming conditions demonstrate that MAPLE-Sec sustains detection across mobility events, improving the converged F1-score by up to 9.3\% relative to Cloud-only and by 2.9 percentage points over the strongest edge-collaborative baseline (EdgeShard), while reducing end-to-end latency by up to 2.5$\times$. Ablation confirms that the activation-compression codec reduces migration energy by 2.4$\times$ versus the raw-activation migration variant.

</details>

Abstract: Integrated sensing and communication allows wireless networks to recognize human activity from the channel estimates that the receiver computes anyway. The inference, however, runs on the general-purpose processors of the base station, access point or handset, which are the first components a device switches off to save energy. Always-on sensing therefore requires a classifier that is small enough to remain powered while the rest of the system sleeps. We investigate whether Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) can provide such a classifier. To this end, we generate SNN accelerators for micro-Doppler activity recognition on the public DISC dataset (IEEE 802.11ay at 60 GHz) with the open-source Spiker+ framework, and implement them together with a spike encoder on a ZCU208 RFSoC, so that the complete chain from micro-Doppler frame to activity label runs in programmable logic. A fully connected SNN with 132 neurons achieves 94.7 % accuracy (90.7 % macro-F1) on unseen subjects and performs an inference in 206 microseconds. Compared to conventional networks of the same topology on the same device, it requires an eighth of the logic and 3.4X less dynamic energy per decision than a network fed the same binary spikes, at comparable accuracy, and 7.6X less than a network fed the 16-bit spectrogram, at 1.2 points lower accuracy. Quantization-aware training reduces its weights to 3 bits within half a percentage point of the 6-bit network at the same encoder threshold
